Exact Separation Logic (Extended Version)
Petar Maksimović, Caroline Cronjäger, Andreas Lööw, Julian Sutherland, Philippa Gardner

Abstract
Over-approximating (OX) program logics, such as separation logic (SL), are used for verifying properties of heap-manipulating programs: all terminating behaviour is characterised, but established results and errors need not be reachable. OX function specifications are thus incompatible with true bug-finding supported by symbolic execution tools such as Pulse and Pulse-X. In contrast, under-approximating (UX) program logics, such as incorrectness separation logic, are used to find true results and bugs: established results and errors are reachable, but there is no mechanism for understanding if all terminating behaviour has been characterised. We introduce exact separation logic (ESL), which provides fully-verified function specifications compatible with both OX verification and UX true bug-funding: all terminating behaviour is characterised and all established results and errors are reachable. We prove soundness for ESL with mutually recursive functions, demonstrating, for the first time, function compositionality for a UX logic. We show that UX program logics require subtle definitions of internal and external function specifications compared with the familiar definitions of OX logics. We investigate the expressivity of ESL and, for the first time, explore the role of abstraction in UX reasoning by verifying abstract ESL specifications of various data-structure algorithms. In doing so, we highlight the difference between abstraction (hiding information) and over-approximation (losing information). Our findings demonstrate that abstraction cannot be used as freely in UX logics as in OX logics, but also that it should be feasible to use ESL to provide tractable function specifications for self-contained, critical code, which would then be used for both verification and true bug-finding.

Nethermind, United Kingdom

1
Introduction

Over-approximating (OX) program logics were introduced to reason about program correctness, starting with Hoare logic
[
18
]
and evolving to separation logic (SL)
[
26
,
29
]
.
SL is used for
verification
and features function specifications of the form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array}\right\}}\ {f(\vec{\mathsf{x}})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q\end{array}\right\}}
, the meaning of which is that all terminating executions of the function
f
that start from a state in the pre-condition
P
end in a state covered by the post-condition
Q
.
SL has the standard rule of
forward consequence
, which allows one to
lose information
(for example, if we had a post-condition with
x=42
, we could soundly weaken this precise information to the less precise
x>0
or even to the non-informative
{\sf true}
).
In essence, the philosophy underlying the OX approach in general can be stated as:

no paths can be cut, but information can be lost.

A key property of SL is that function specifications are
compositional
, enabling
scalable
reasoning about the heap. This is due to their
locality
, which allows the
pre-condition to describe only the partial state sufficient for the function to execute,
and the
frame
property, which allows the function to be called in any larger state.
SL function specifications have been used for verification of complex, real-world code in tools such as VeriFast
[
19
]
, Iris
[
20
]
, and Gillian
[
10
,
23
]
. However, given that their post-conditions may describe states that are not reachable from their pre-conditions, such OX specifications are not compatible with true bug-finding, as found, for example, in Meta’s Pulse
[
27
]
and Pulse-X
[
21
]
tools.

Under-approximating (UX) program logics were recently introduced, originating from reverse Hoare logic (RHL)
[
8
]

for reasoning about correctness of probabilistic programs, and coming to prominence with incorrectness logic
[
25
]
and incorrectness separation logic (ISL)
[
27
]
, which identified
their bug-finding potential. ISL function specifications are of the form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array}\right]}\ {f(\vec{\mathsf{x}})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array}\right]}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array}\right]}\ {f(\vec{\mathsf{x}})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}\right]}
, the meaning of which is that any state in the success post-condition
Q_{\mathit{ok}}
or the error post-condition
Q_{\mathit{err}}
is reachable from some state in the pre-condition
P
by executing the function
f
; this guarantees that all results and bugs reported in the post-conditions will be true. In contrast to SL, ISL uses the rule of
backward consequence
, which allows one to
cut paths
(for example, if we had a post-condition with
x>0
, we could soundly strengthen this information to consider only the path in which
x=42
). Therefore, the philosophy underlying UX logics in general can be summarised as:

paths can be cut, but no information can be lost.

When it comes to the use of ISL function specifications, whilst this has been implemented in Pulse-X, as far as we are aware, ISL does not feature function-call rules,
and function compositionality for ISL and UX logics has not been proven. Moreover, as it is not possible to determine if UX specifications cover all terminating behaviour, they remain incompatible with verification and cannot therefore be used in tools such as VeriFast, Iris, and Gillian.

Our challenge is to develop a program logic in which we can state and prove function specifications that are compatible with
both
verification and true bug-finding.
Our motivation comes from the unique flexibility and expressivity that such specifications would provide, as they could be used by verification and bug-finding tools alike, closing the gap between these two contrasting paradigms. From our experience in program logics and associated tool-building, we believe that the main use case for exact specification should be self-contained, critical code, such as widely-used data-structure libraries.

We introduce exact separation logic (ESL), with
exact
(EX) function specifications of the form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array}\right)}\ {f(\vec{\mathsf{x}})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}\right)}
, whose meaning combines that of SL and ISL specifications:
all terminating executions of the function that start from a state in the pre-condition
P
end in a state covered by the post-conditions;
and
all states in two post-conditions are reachable from a state in the pre-condition by executing the function. The exactness of ESL can be captured by the slogan:

no paths can be cut and no information can be lost.

The slogan is supported by the rule of
equivalence
, which combines the forward consequence of SL and the backward consequence of ISL. In fact, ESL proof rules form a common core of SL and ISL, and ESL should therefore be a familiar setting to those acquainted with either.

We prove soundness for ESL with mutually recursive functions, which we believe is the first proof of function compositionality for a UX logic, and which transfers immediately to ISL. In doing so, drawing inspiration from InsecSL
[
24
]
, we provide formal definitions of
external
and
internal
function specifications, which describe, respectively, the interface a function exposes towards its clients and towards its implementation, and highlight the difference in complexity between these two types of specifications in OX and UX reasoning.

Using numerous examples, we demonstrate (in the main text and in Appendix
F
) how ESL can be used to reason about data-structure libraries, language errors, mutual recursion, and non-termination. In doing so, we introduce, for the first time, abstract predicates to UX reasoning and provide abstract function specifications for a number of data-structure algorithms, focussing on singly-linked lists and binary trees. In doing so, we highlight an important difference between the concepts of abstraction and over-approximation: in particular, abstraction corresponds to
hiding
information whereas over-approximation corresponds to
losing
it. Our findings demonstrate that, while abstraction cannot be used as freely in UX logics as in OX logics, sometimes resulting in less abstract specifications and more complex proofs, it should be feasible to use ESL to provide tractable function specifications for self-contained, critical code that can then be used for both verification and true bug-finding.

2
Exact Separation Logic by Example

We guide the reader through what it means to write ESL specifications and proofs by intuition and example, contrasting our findings with those known from SL and ISL.

Illustrative Example.

Consider the command
C\triangleq\mathtt{if}\ (\mathsf{x}>0)\ \{\mathsf{y}\mathtt{\ :=\ }42\}\ \mathtt{else}\ \{\mathsf{y}\mathtt{\ :=\ }21\}
, which can be specified, starting from the pre-condition
\mathsf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}
, in ESL, SL, and ISL as follows:

\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}\end{array}~)}\\
\mathtt{if}\ (\mathsf{x}>0)\ \{\\
\quad\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{x}>0\end{array}~)}\\
\quad\!\!\mathsf{y}\mathtt{\ :=\ }42\\
\quad\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}Q_{1}:\mathsf{x}>0\land\mathsf{y}=42\end{array}~)}\\
\}\ \mathtt{else}\ \{\\
\quad\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{x}\leq 0\end{array}~)}\\
\quad\!\!\mathsf{y}\mathtt{\ :=\ }21\\
\quad\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}Q_{2}:\mathsf{x}\leq 0\land\mathsf{y}=21\end{array}~\right)}\\
\}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}Q_{1}\lor Q_{2}\end{array}~)}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left\{~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}\end{array}~\right\}}\\
\mathtt{if}\ (\mathsf{x}>0)\ \{\\
\quad\!\!\ldots\\
\quad\!\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\text{// Same as ESL}}\\
\quad\!\!\ldots\\
\}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left\{~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}Q_{1}\lor Q_{2}\end{array}~\right\}}\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\text{// Losing information }}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left\{~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}\land\mathsf{y}>0\end{array}~\right\}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}\end{array}~\right]}\\
\mathtt{if}\ (\mathsf{x}>0)\ \{\\
\quad\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{x}>0\end{array}~\right]}\\
\quad\!\!\mathsf{y}\mathtt{\ :=\ }42\\
\quad\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}Q_{1}:\mathsf{x}>0\land\mathsf{y}=42\end{array}~\right]}\\
\}\ \mathtt{else}\ \{~\mathsf{y}\mathtt{\ :=\ }21~\}\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\text{// Path cutting}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{x}>0\land\mathsf{y}=42\end{array}~\right]}\end{array}

As ESL specifications must neither cut paths nor lose information (in this example, about the values of
\mathsf{x}
and
\mathsf{y}
), the ESL post-condition of
C
must be equivalent to
(\mathsf{x}>0\land\mathsf{y}=42)\lor(\mathsf{x}\leq 0\land\mathsf{y}=21)
. In SL, it is possible to use forward consequence to weaken this information and obtain, for example,
\mathsf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}\land\mathsf{y}>0
, or just
\mathsf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}
, or even just
{\sf true}
.
In ISL, it is possible to cut, for example, the
\mathtt{else}
branch of the
\mathtt{if}
statement, but the values of
\mathsf{x}
and
\mathsf{y}
must be maintained in the post-condition of the
\mathtt{then}
branch,
\mathsf{x}>0\land\mathsf{y}=42
.

One question that we have been often asked is whether it is simpler to prove an exact specification
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array}\right)}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}\right)}
in ESL, or to prove it separately in SL and ISL. The answer is that it is simpler to prove the specification in ESL. If a specification is exact, then it does not cut paths and it does not lose information. Therefore, the tools that make SL and ISL proofs simpler than ESL proofs, namely forward consequence and backward consequence, can only be used in very limited ways, if at all.
From our experience, the ISL proof of an exact specification will turn out to be almost identical to the ESL one, and an SL proof on top of that would duplicate a large part of the work. In fact, if one were to try to prove the exact specification
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}\end{array})}\ {C}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}(\mathsf{x}>0\land\mathsf{y}=42)\lor(\mathsf{x}\leq 0\land\mathsf{y}=21)\end{array})}
from the above example in either SL or ISL, they would obtain exactly the same proof as in ESL.

We also emphasise that ESL is not meant to replace either SL or ISL. If one is interested in only verification or only bug-finding, then one should use a formalism tailored to that type of analysis to exploit the available shortcuts. However, if one wanted to use the same codebase for both verification and bug-finding, then ESL offers a way of providing specifications useful for both. One example of such a codebase would be a widely-used data-structure library, where some of the users use it for verification and others for bug-finding.

List-length in ESL: Intuition.

We consider a list-length function,
\mathtt{LLen}(\mathsf{x})
, which takes a list at
\mathsf{x}
, does not modify it, and returns its length, and the following
ESL specification:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,n)\end{array})}\ {~\mathtt{LLen}(\mathsf{x})~}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(ok:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{list}(x,n)\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=n\end{array})}

This specification uses a standard list-length predicate,
\mathsf{list}(x,n)
, which states that the length of the list at
x
equals
n
and is defined as follows:

\mathsf{list}(x,n)\triangleq(x=\mathtt{null}\mathrel{\star}n=0)\lor(\exists v,x^{\prime}\ldotp x\mapsto v,x^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x^{\prime},n-1)),

hiding the information about the values and internal node addresses of the list. Before proving this specification, we establish some intuition about why it holds. Let us assume that it does not hold and try to find a counter-example: by the meaning of ESL specifications, it is either not OX-valid or it is not UX-valid. The former, however, is not possible, as the analogous SL specification holds. The latter means that it is possible to
find a state in the post-condition not reachable by the execution of
f
from any state in the pre-condition, and may be unfamiliar to the reader as UX program logics have been introduced only recently.

We start looking for such a state in the post-condition (post-model) by choosing some values for
x
and
n
: say,
x=0
and
n=2
. This also fixes
\mathsf{ret}
to 2.
Then, we fully unfold
\mathsf{list}(0,2)
to obtain
\exists v_{1},x_{1},v_{2}.~0\mapsto v_{1},x_{1}\mathrel{\star}x_{1}\mapsto v_{2},\mathtt{null}
, and instantiate the existentials
v_{1}
,
x_{1}
, and
v_{2}
: say, with
1
,
4
, and
9
, respectively. In this way, we obtain the state described by the assertion
0\mapsto 1,4\mathrel{\star}4\mapsto 9,\mathtt{null}
.
When it comes to the pre-condition,
x
and
n
(and also
\mathsf{x}
) are fixed by the post-model choices, and when we unfold the list, the pre-condition becomes
\mathsf{x}=0\mathrel{\star}\exists v_{1},x_{1},v_{2}.~0\mapsto v_{1},x_{1}\mathrel{\star}x_{1}\mapsto v_{2},\mathtt{null}
. As the algorithm does not modify the list, it becomes clear that if we choose
v_{1}
,
x_{1}
, and
v_{2}
as for the post-model (that is, 1, 4, and 9, respectively), the algorithm will reach our post-model. Given that the same reasoning would apply for any choice of
x
and
n
, we realise that the given specification is, in fact, also UX-valid and hence exact. This reveals an important observation, which is that

abstraction does not always equate to over-approximation, that is,

hiding information does not always mean losing information.

For those used to OX reasoning, it might appear that the post-condition
\mathsf{list}(x,n)\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=n
loses information about the structure of the list, but the insight here is that this information was never known in the pre-condition in the first place, as we also only had
\mathsf{list}(x,n)
there.

List-length in ESL: Proof Sketch.

Reasoning about function specifications in the UX/EX setting has not been studied previously and requires subtle definitions of
external function specifications
, which provide the interface that the function exposes to the client, and
internal function specifications
, which provide the interface to the function’s implementation. With OX logics, these are well-understood and the gap between them is small. For UX/EX logics, this gap is larger. We illustrate these concepts informally using the list-length example, and give the corresponding formal definitions in §
4
.

The proof sketch of the ESL external specification of the list-length algorithm is given in Figure
1
.
It is more complex than its SL counterpart (see Appendix
G
), but is manageable and comes with the benefit that this ESL specification can be used for both verification and bug-finding.

First, as the function is recursive, we have to provide a measure and prove the specification extended with this measure: in this case, the measure is
\alpha=n
, given by the length of the list. This measure is necessary to ensure the finite reachability property for mutually recursive functions in UX logics, and is a known technique from the work on total correctness specifications for OX logics
[
9
,
7
]
. Recursive function calls are then allowed only if they use specifications of a strictly smaller measure, represented in the proof sketch by the function specification context
\Gamma(\alpha)
, which contains the specification of
\mathtt{LLen}(\mathsf{x})
for all
\beta<\alpha
.

The move from the external to the internal pre-condition initialises the local function variables to
\mathtt{null}
. The ESL rule for the
\mathtt{if}
statement, just like in SL, adds the condition to the then-branch, its negation to the else-branch, and collects the branch post-conditions using disjunction. The rules for the basic commands (here, the assignments
\mathsf{r}\mathtt{\ :=\ }0
and
\mathsf{r}\mathtt{\ :=\ }\mathsf{r}+1
and the lookup
\mathsf{x}\mathtt{\ :=\ }[\mathsf{x}+1]
) are also the same as in SL, as these are already exact.
The unfolding of the list is also done in the same way, as unfolding always preserves equivalence; note how the condition of the
\mathtt{if}
statement determines the appropriate disjunct for the list predicate. The recursive function call is allowed to go through as it is used with measure
n-1
(with the parts of the assertion representing the pre- and the post-condition highlighted).

The major difference between ISL/ESL and SL proofs is that we cannot lose
information about the function parameters and local variables in the middle of the former. Therefore, we cannot simplify the assertions
Q^{\prime}_{1}
and
Q^{\prime}_{2}
further and cannot fold back the list predicate within the internal specification, as we would do in SL (cf. corresponding proof in Appendix
G
).

\small\begin{array}[]{r@{~}l}&\hskip-32.72049pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\text{// Function is recursive and requires a measure: $\alpha=n$}}\\
\Gamma(\alpha)\vdash&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,n)\mathrel{\star}\alpha=n\end{array}~)}\\
&\mathtt{LLen}(\mathsf{x})~\{\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\text{// Transition from external to internal pre-condition: initialise locals to $\mathtt{null}$}}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,n)\mathrel{\star}\alpha=n\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{r}=\mathtt{null}\end{array}~)}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\mathtt{if}\ (\mathsf{x}=\mathtt{null})\ \{\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,n)\mathrel{\star}\alpha=n\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{r}=\mathtt{null}\mathrel{\star}{\mathsf{x}=\mathtt{null}}\end{array}~)}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\mathsf{r}\mathtt{\ :=\ }0\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}Q^{\prime}_{1}:\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,n)\mathrel{\star}\alpha=n\mathrel{\star}{\mathsf{r}=0}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{x}=\mathtt{null}\end{array}~)}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\}\ \mathtt{else}\ \{\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\hbox{\pagecolor{slgray}$\mathsf{list}(x,n)$}\mathrel{\star}\alpha=n\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{r}=\mathtt{null}\mathrel{\star}\hbox{\pagecolor{slgray}$\mathsf{x}\mathbin{\neq}\mathtt{null}$}\end{array}~)}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}// Unfold $\mathsf{list}(x,n)$ using the equivalence }}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}// $\models\mathsf{list}(\mathsf{x},n)\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{x}\mathbin{\neq}\mathtt{null}\Leftrightarrow\exists v,x^{\prime}.~\mathsf{x}\mapsto v,x^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x^{\prime},n-1)$}}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\hbox{\pagecolor{slgray}$\exists v,x^{\prime}$}.~\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\hbox{\pagecolor{slgray}$x\mapsto v,x^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x^{\prime},n-1)$}\mathrel{\star}\alpha=n\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{r}=\mathtt{null}\end{array}~)}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\mathsf{x}\mathtt{\ :=\ }[\mathsf{x}+1];\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\exists v,x^{\prime}.~\mathsf{x}=x^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}x\mapsto v,x^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x^{\prime},n-1)\mathrel{\star}\alpha=n\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{r}=\mathtt{null}\end{array}~)}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}// As $\alpha-1<\alpha$, we can use the specification of $\mathsf{LLen}(\mathsf{x})$ with measure $\alpha-1$}}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\exists v,x^{\prime}.~\hbox{\pagecolor{slgray}$\mathsf{x}=x^{\prime}$}\mathrel{\star}x\mapsto v,x^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}\hbox{\pagecolor{slgray}$\mathsf{list}(x^{\prime},n-1)\mathrel{\star}\alpha-1=n-1$}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{r}=\mathtt{null}\end{array}~)}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\mathsf{r}\mathtt{\ :=\ }\mathtt{LLen}(\mathsf{x});\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\exists v,x^{\prime}.~\mathsf{x}=x^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}x\mapsto v,x^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x^{\prime},n-1)\mathrel{\star}\alpha-1=n-1\mathrel{\star}\hbox{\pagecolor{slgray}$\mathsf{r}=n-1$}\end{array}~)}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\mathsf{r}\mathtt{\ :=\ }\mathsf{r}+1\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}Q_{2}^{\prime}:\exists v,x^{\prime}.~\mathsf{x}=x^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}x\mapsto v,x^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x^{\prime},n-1)\mathrel{\star}\alpha-1=n-1\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{r}=n\end{array}~)}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\};\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}Q^{\prime}:Q_{1}^{\prime}\lor Q_{2}^{\prime}\end{array}~)}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\mathtt{return}\ \mathsf{r}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}Q^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=\mathsf{r}\end{array}~)}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\text{// Transition from internal to external post-condition given in text}}\\
&\}\\
&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{list}(x,n)\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=n\mathrel{\star}\alpha=n\end{array}~)}\end{array}

Figure 1:
ESL proof sketch:
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,n)\end{array})}\ {~\mathtt{LLen}(\mathsf{x})~}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(ok:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{list}(x,n)\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=n\end{array})}
.

The most complex part of the proof sketch is the transition from the internal to the external post-condition, in which we have to somehow forget the local variables of the function, given that they must not spill out into the calling context. This is done by replacing them with fresh, existentially quantified logical variables, which in this case also allows us to use equivalence to fold back the list predicate and reach the target post-condition. The details of this transition, in which we denote
\mathsf{ret}=n\mathrel{\star}\alpha=n
by
R
, are as follows:

\begin{array}[]{@{}l}\exists x_{q},r_{q}\ldotp Q^{\prime}[x_{q}/\mathsf{x}][r_{q}/\mathsf{r}]\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=\mathsf{r}[x_{q}/\mathsf{x}][r_{q}/\mathsf{r}]\\[0.85358pt]
\quad\Leftrightarrow((x=\mathtt{null}\mathrel{\star}n=0)\lor(\exists x_{q},r_{q},v,x^{\prime}.~x_{q}=x^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}x\mapsto v,x^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x^{\prime},n-1)\mathrel{\star}r_{q}=n))\mathrel{\star}R\\
\quad\Leftrightarrow((x=\mathtt{null}\mathrel{\star}n=0)\lor(\exists v,x^{\prime}.~x\mapsto v,x^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x^{\prime},n-1)))\mathrel{\star}R~\text{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}// can fold now}}\\
\quad\Leftrightarrow\mathsf{list}(x,n)\mathrel{\star}(n=0\lor n\mathbin{>}0)\mathrel{\star}R\\
\quad\Leftrightarrow\mathsf{list}(x,n)\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=n\mathrel{\star}\alpha=n\end{array}

Observe that, since we are proving an EX specification, we are not allowed to cut paths. This means that the ISL proof of the analogous ISL specification of
\texttt{LLen}(\mathsf{x})
would be identical, noting that the use of equivalence would technically be replaced by backward consequence.

List-insert in ESL: Intuition.

The list-length function,
\texttt{LLen}(\mathsf{x})
, is an example of an algorithm where the EX specification is analogous to the traditional OX specification. At times, however, ESL specifications have to be more complex.
Consider, for example, the list-insert algorithm
\texttt{LInsertFirst}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{v})
, which inserts the element
\mathsf{v}
at the beginning of the list
\mathsf{x}
. Its traditional OX specification is:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{v}=v\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{vs})\end{array}\}}\ {~\mathtt{LInsertFirst}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{v})~}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{list}(\mathsf{ret},v:\mathit{vs})\end{array}\}}

where
\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{vs})
is the standard list predicate that exposes the values of the list:

\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{vs})\triangleq(x=\mathtt{null}\mathrel{\star}\mathit{vs}=[~])\lor(\exists v,x^{\prime},\mathit{vs}^{\prime}\ldotp x\mapsto v,x^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x^{\prime},\mathit{vs}^{\prime})\mathrel{\star}\mathit{vs}=v:\mathit{vs}^{\prime})

Using the counter-example approach to check if this specification is EX-valid, we easily see that it loses information: in particular, no end-state where
x
is not the
second
pointer in the returned list
\mathsf{ret}
is reachable from the given pre-condition.
Consequently, for EX validity, we are required to use the following, less abstract, ESL specification for
LInsertFirst
:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{v}=v\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs},\mathit{vs})\end{array})}\ {~\mathtt{LInsertFirst}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{v})~}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{list}(\mathsf{ret},\mathsf{ret}:\mathit{xs},v:\mathit{vs})\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{listHead}(x,\mathit{xs})\end{array})}\\

where
\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs},\mathit{vs})
is a predicate that exposes the internal pointers of a given list in addition to the values, and
\mathsf{listHead}(x,\mathit{xs})
states that the list
\mathit{xs}
starts with
x
.

Further Examples.

In §
5
and Appendix
F
, we give many additional examples of ESL specifications and proofs to illustrate reasoning about
list algorithms and binary trees, as well as language errors, mutual recursion, non-termination, and client programs.

3
The Programming Language

We introduce ESL using a simple programming language, the syntax of which is given below.

Language Syntax

\begin{array}[]{r@{~~}c@{~~}l}v\in\mathsf{Val}&::=&n\in\mathsf{Nat}\mid b\in\mathsf{Bool}\mid s\in\mathsf{Str}\mid\mathtt{null}\mid\overline{v}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 9.24994pt~\mathsf{x}\in\mathsf{PVar}\\
\mathsf{E}\in\mathsf{PExp}&::=&v\mid\mathsf{x}\mid\mathsf{E}+\mathsf{E}\mid\mathsf{E}-\mathsf{E}\mid...\mid\mathsf{E}=\mathsf{E}\mid\mathsf{E}<\mathsf{E}\mid\neg\ \mathsf{E}\mid\mathsf{E}\wedge\mathsf{E}\mid...\mid\mathsf{E}:\mathsf{E}\mid\mathsf{E}\mathbin{@}\mathsf{E}\mid...\\[5.69054pt]
\mathsf{C}\in\mathsf{Cmd}&::=&\mathtt{skip}\mid\mathsf{x}\mathtt{\ :=\ }\mbox{$\mathsf{E}$}\mid\mathsf{x}\mathtt{\ :=\ }\mathtt{nondet}\mid\mathtt{error}(\mathsf{E})\mid\mathtt{if}\ (\mathsf{E})\ \mathsf{C}\ \mathtt{else}\ \mathsf{C}\mid\mathtt{while}\ (\mathsf{E})\ \mathsf{C}\mid\mathsf{C};\mathsf{C}\mid\\
&&\mathsf{y}\mathtt{\ :=\ }f(\vec{\mbox{$\mathsf{E}$}})\mid\mathsf{x}\mathtt{\ :=\ }[\mbox{$\mathsf{E}$}]\mid[\mbox{$\mathsf{E}$}]\mathtt{\ :=\ }\mbox{$\mathsf{E}$}\mid\mathsf{x}\mathtt{\ :=\ }\mathtt{new}(\mathsf{E})\mid\mathtt{free}(\mbox{$\mathsf{E}$})\end{array}

Values,
v\in\mathsf{Val}
, include: natural numbers,
n\in\mathsf{Nat}
; Booleans,
b\in\mathsf{Bool}\triangleq

\{{\sf true},{\sf false}\}
; strings,
s\in\mathsf{Str}
; a dedicated value

\mathtt{null}
; and lists of values,
\overline{v}\in\mathsf{List}
. Expressions,
\mathsf{E}\in\mathsf{Exp}
, comprise values, program variables,
\mathsf{x}\in\mathsf{PVar}
, and various unary and binary operators (e.g., addition, equality, negation, conjunction, list prepending, and list concatenation).
Commands comprise:
the variable assignment; non-deterministic number generation; error raising;
the
\mathtt{if}
statement; the
\mathtt{while}
loop; command sequencing;
function call; and memory management commands, that is, lookup,
mutation, allocation, and deallocation. The sets of program variables
for expressions and commands, denoted by
\mathtt{pv}(\mathsf{E})
and
\mathtt{pv}(\mathsf{C})

respectively, and the
sets of modified variables for commands, denoted by
\mathtt{mod}(\mathsf{C})
, are
defined in the standard way.

Definition 1

(Functions)
.

A function, denoted by

f(\vec{\mathsf{x}})\,\{\,\mathsf{C};\mathtt{return}\ \mathsf{E}\,\}
, comprises:
a function identifier,
f\in\mathsf{Str}
; the function
parameters,
\vec{\mathsf{x}}
, given by
a list of distinct program variables; a function
body,
\mathsf{C}\in\mathsf{Cmd}
; and a return expression,
\mathsf{E}\in\mathsf{PExp}
, with
\mathtt{pv}(\mathsf{E})\subseteq\{\vec{\mathsf{x}}\}\cup\mathtt{pv}(\mathsf{C})
.

Program variables in function bodies that are not the function
parameters are treated as local variables initialised to
\mathtt{null}
, with their scope not
extending beyond the function.

Definition 2

(Function Implementation Contexts)
.

A function implementation context,
\gamma:\mathsf{Str}\rightharpoonup_{\mathit{fin}}\mathsf{PVar}~\mathsf{List}\times\mathsf{Cmd}\times\mathsf{PExp}
, is a finite partial function from function identifiers to their implementations. For
\gamma(f)=(\vec{\mathsf{x}},\mathsf{C},{\mathsf{E}})
, we also write

f(\vec{\mathsf{x}})\{\mathsf{C};\mathtt{return}\ \mathsf{E}\}\in\gamma
.

We next define an operational semantics that gives a complete account of
the behaviour of commands and does not get stuck on any input, as
we explicitly account for language errors and missing resource errors.

Definition 3

(Stores, Heaps, States)
.

Variable stores,
s:\mathsf{PVar}\rightharpoonup_{\mathtt{fin}}\mathsf{Val}
, are partial finite functions from program variables to values. Heaps,
h:\mathsf{Nat}\rightharpoonup_{\mathtt{fin}}(\mathsf{Val}\uplus\varnothing)
, are partial finite
functions from natural numbers to values extended with a dedicated
symbol
\varnothing\notin\mathsf{Val}
. Program states,
\sigma=(s,h)
, consist of a store and a heap.

Heaps are used to model the memory, and the dedicated
symbol
\varnothing\notin\mathsf{Val}
is required for UX frame preservation
1
1

1

UX frame preservation means that if a program runs with a non-missing outcome to a given final state, then it also runs with the same outcome to an extended final state, with the extension (the
frame
) unaffected by the execution. From ISL
[
27
]
, it is known that losing deallocation information breaks UX frame preservation; the solution is to keep track of deallocated cells, which we achieve by using
\varnothing
.
to hold
(cf. Definition
10
). In particular,
h(n)=v
means that an
allocated heap cell with address
n
contains the value
v
;
and
h(n)=\varnothing
means that a heap cell with address
n
has been
deallocated
[
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
27
]
. This linear memory model is used in much of the
SL literature, including
ISL
[
27
]
.
Onward,
\emptyset
denotes the empty heap,
h_{1}\uplus h_{2}
denotes heap disjoint union, and
h_{1}\mathbin{\sharp}h_{2}
denotes that
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
are disjoint.

Definition 4

(Expression Evaluation)
.

The evaluation of an expression
\mathsf{E}
with respect to a store
s
,
denoted
\llbracket{\mathsf{E}}\rrbracket_{s}
,
results in either a value or a dedicated symbol denoting an
evaluation error,
\lightning\notin\mathsf{Val}
.
Some illustrative
cases are:

\llbracket{v}\rrbracket_{s}=v\quad\llbracket{\mathsf{x}}\rrbracket_{s}=\begin{cases}s(\mathsf{x}),&\mbox{$\mathsf{x}\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}(s)$}\\
{\lightning},&\mbox{otherwise}\end{cases}\quad\llbracket{\mathsf{E}_{1}~{+}~\mathsf{E}_{2}}\rrbracket_{s}=\begin{cases}\llbracket{\mathsf{E}_{1}}\rrbracket_{s}~{+}~\llbracket{\mathsf{E}_{2}}\rrbracket_{s},&\mbox{$\llbracket{\mathsf{E}_{1}}\rrbracket_{s},\llbracket{\mathsf{E}_{2}}\rrbracket_{s}\in\mathsf{Nat}$}\\
{\lightning},&\mbox{otherwise}\end{cases}

The big-step operational semantics uses judgements of the form

\sigma,\mathsf{C}\Downarrow_{\gamma}o:{\sigma^{\prime}}
, read: given implementation context

\gamma
and starting from state
\sigma
, the execution of command
\mathsf{C}

results in outcome
o\in O={\{\mathit{ok},\mathit{err},\mathit{miss}\}}
and
state
\sigma^{\prime}
. The outcome can
either equal:
\mathit{ok}
(elided where possible), denoting a
successful execution;
\mathit{err}
, denoting an execution
faulting with a language error, or
\mathit{miss}
, denoting an
execution faulting with a missing resource error.

Definition 5

(Operational Semantics)
.

The representative cases of the big-step operational semantics are given in
Figure
2
. The complete semantics is given in Appendix
A
.

The successful transitions are straightforward: for
example, the
\mathsf{nondet}
command generates an arbitrary natural number; the
function call executes the function body in a
store where the function parameters are given the values of the function arguments
and the function locals are initialised to
\mathtt{null}
;
and the control flow statements behave as expected. Allocation
requires the specified amount of contiguous cells (always available
as heaps are finite), and lookup, mutation, and deallocation
require the targeted cell not to have been freed.

The semantics stores error information in a dedicated program variable
\mathsf{err}
, not available to the programmer. For simplicity of error messages, we assume to have a function
\mathsf{str}:\mathsf{PExp}\rightarrow\mathsf{Str}
, which serialises program expressions into strings. The faulting semantic transitions are split into
language errors
, which can be captured by program-logic reasoning, and

missing resource errors
, which cannot, as such errors break the frame property. Language errors arise due to, for example, expressions being incorrectly typed
(e.g.
\mathtt{null}+1
) or an attempt to access deallocated cells (that is, the use-after-free error).
On the other hand, missing resource errors arise from accessing cells that are not present in memory.

({s},{h}),\mathsf{x}\mathtt{\ :=\ }\mathsf{E}\Downarrow_{\gamma}({s^{\prime}},{h})\llbracket{\mathsf{E}}\rrbracket_{s}=v\hskip 9.24994pts^{\prime}=s[\mathsf{x}\rightarrow v]\hskip 18.49988pt({s},{h}),\mathsf{x}\mathtt{\ :=\ }\mathtt{nondet}\Downarrow_{\gamma}({s^{\prime}},{h})n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 9.24994pts^{\prime}=s[\mathsf{x}\rightarrow n]\hskip 18.49988pt({s},{h}),\mathtt{while}\ (\mathsf{E})\ \mathsf{C}\Downarrow_{\gamma}({s},{h})\llbracket{\mathsf{E}}\rrbracket_{s}={\sf false}\\
({s},{h}),\mathtt{while}\ (\mathsf{E})\ \mathsf{C}\Downarrow_{\gamma}o:\sigma^{\prime}\begin{array}[]{c}\llbracket{\mathsf{E}}\rrbracket_{s}={\sf true}\hskip 9.24994pt({s},{h}),\mathsf{C}\Downarrow_{\gamma}\sigma^{\prime\prime}\\
\sigma^{\prime\prime},\mathtt{while}\ (\mathsf{E})\ \mathsf{C}\Downarrow_{\gamma}o:\sigma^{\prime}\end{array}\hskip 9.24994pt({s},{h}),\mathsf{y}\mathtt{\ :=\ }f(\vec{\mathsf{E}})\Downarrow_{\gamma}({s[\mathsf{y}\rightarrow v^{\prime}]},{h^{\prime}})\begin{array}[]{c}f(\vec{\mathsf{x}})\,\{\,\mathsf{C};\mathtt{return}\ \mathsf{E}^{\prime}\,\}\in\gamma\hskip 9.24994pt\llbracket{\vec{\mathsf{E}}}\rrbracket_{s}=\vec{v}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{pv}(\mathsf{C})\setminus\{\vec{\mathsf{x}}\}=\{\vec{\mathsf{z}}\}\\
s_{p}=\emptyset[\vec{\mathsf{x}}\rightarrow\vec{v}][\vec{\mathsf{z}}\rightarrow\mathtt{null}]\hskip 9.24994pt(s_{p},h),\mathsf{C}\Downarrow_{\gamma}({s_{q}},{h^{\prime}})\hskip 9.24994pt\llbracket{\mathsf{E}^{\prime}}\rrbracket_{s_{q}}=v^{\prime}\end{array}\\
({s},{h}),\mathsf{x}\mathtt{\ :=\ }[\mathsf{E}]\Downarrow_{\gamma}({s[\mathsf{x}\rightarrow v]},{h})\llbracket{\mathsf{E}}\rrbracket_{s}=n\hskip 9.24994pth(n)=v\hskip 18.49988pt({s},{h}),[\mathsf{E}_{1}]\mathtt{\ :=\ }\mathsf{E}_{2}\Downarrow_{\gamma}({s},{h^{\prime}})\llbracket{\mathsf{E}_{1}}\rrbracket_{s}=n\hskip 9.24994pth(n)\in\mathsf{Val}\hskip 9.24994pt\llbracket{\mathsf{E}_{2}}\rrbracket_{s}=v\hskip 9.24994pth^{\prime}=h[n\mapsto v]\\
({s},{h}),\mathsf{x}\mathtt{\ :=\ }\mathtt{new}(\mathsf{E})\Downarrow_{\gamma}({s[\mathsf{x}\rightarrow n^{\prime}]},{h^{\prime}})\begin{array}[]{c}(n^{\prime}+i\notin\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}(h))|_{i=1}^{\llbracket{\mathsf{E}}\rrbracket_{s}-1}\hskip 9.24994pth^{\prime}=h\uplus\{(n^{\prime}+i\mapsto\mathtt{null})|_{i=1}^{\llbracket{\mathsf{E}}\rrbracket_{s}-1}\}\end{array}\hskip 9.24994pt({s},{h}),\mathtt{free}(\mathsf{E})\Downarrow_{\gamma}({s},{h[n\mapsto\varnothing]})\llbracket{\mathsf{E}}\rrbracket_{s}=n\hskip 9.24994pth(n)\in\mathsf{Val}\\
({s},{h}),\mathsf{x}\mathtt{\ :=\ }[\mathsf{E}]\Downarrow_{\gamma}{\mathit{err}}:({s_{\mathit{err}}},{h})\begin{array}[]{c}\llbracket{\mathsf{E}}\rrbracket_{s}=\lightning\hskip 9.24994ptv_{\mathit{err}}=[``\mathsf{ExprEval}",\mathsf{str}(\mathsf{E})]\end{array}\hskip 18.49988pt({s},{h}),\mathsf{x}\mathtt{\ :=\ }[\mathsf{E}]\Downarrow_{\gamma}{\mathit{miss}}:({s_{\mathit{err}}},{h})\begin{array}[]{c}\llbracket{\mathsf{E}}\rrbracket_{s}=n\notin\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}(h)\hskip 9.24994ptv_{\mathit{err}}=[``\mathsf{MissingCell}",\mathsf{str}(\mathsf{E}),n]\end{array}\\
({s},{h}),\mathsf{x}\mathtt{\ :=\ }[\mathsf{E}]\Downarrow_{\gamma}{\mathit{err}}:({s_{\mathit{err}}},{h})h(\llbracket{\mathsf{E}}\rrbracket_{s})=\varnothing\hskip 9.24994ptv_{\mathit{err}}=[``\mathsf{UseAfterFree}",\mathsf{str}(\mathsf{E}_{1}),\llbracket{\mathsf{E}}\rrbracket_{s}]\hskip 18.49988pt({s},{h}),\mathtt{error}(\mathsf{E})\Downarrow_{\gamma}\mathit{err}:({s_{\mathit{err}}},{h})\llbracket{\mathsf{E}}\rrbracket_{s}=v\hskip 9.24994ptv_{\mathit{err}}=[``\mathsf{Error}",v]

Figure 2:
Operational semantics (excerpt),
with
s_{\mathit{err}}\triangleq s[\mathsf{err}\rightarrow v_{\mathit{err}}]
and

\mathsf{str}:\mathsf{PExp}\rightarrow\mathsf{Str}
.

4
Exact Separation Logic

We introduce an exact separation logic for our programming language,
giving the assertion language in §
4.1
, specifications in §
4.2
, and the program logic rules in §
4.3
.

4.1
Assertion Language

To define assertions and their meaning, we introduce
logical variables
,
x,y,z,\in\mathsf{LVar}
, distinct from program variables, and define the set of
logical expressions
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{l@{~}l@{~}lr}\mathit{E}\in\mathsf{LExp}&\triangleq&v\mid x\mid\mathsf{x}\mid\mathit{E}+\mathit{E}\mid\mathit{E}-\mathit{E}\mid...\mid\mathit{E}=\mathit{E}\mid\neg\ \mathit{E}\mid\mathit{E}\land\mathit{E}\mid...\mid\mathit{E}\cdot\mathit{E}\mid\mathit{E}:\mathit{E}\mid...\\
\end{array}

Note that we can use program expressions in assertions (for example,
\mathsf{E}\in\mathsf{Val}
), as they form a proper subset of logical expressions.

Definition 6

(Assertion Language)
.

The assertion language is defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{r@{~}c@{~}l}\pi\in\mathsf{BAsrt}&\triangleq&\mathit{E}_{1}=\mathit{E}_{2}\mid\mathit{E}_{1}<\mathit{E}_{2}\mid\mathit{E}\in X\mid\ldots\mid\lnot\pi\mid\pi_{1}\Rightarrow\pi_{2}\par\\
P\in\mathsf{Asrt}&\triangleq&\pi\mid\mathsf{False}\mid P_{1}\Rightarrow P_{2}\mid\exists x\ldotp P\mid{\sf emp}\mid\mathit{E}_{1}\mapsto\mathit{E}_{2}\mid\mathit{E}\mapsto\varnothing\mid P_{1}\mathrel{\star}P_{2}\mid\mathop{\mbox{\Large$\varoast$}}_{\mathit{E}_{1}\leq x<\mathit{E}_{2}}P\end{array}

where
\mathit{E},\mathit{E}_{1},\mathit{E}_{2}\in\mathsf{LExp}
,
X\subseteq\mathsf{Val}
, and
\mathit{x}\in\mathsf{LVar}
.

Boolean assertions,
\pi\in\mathsf{BAsrt}
, lift Boolean logical
expressions to assertions.
Assertions,
P\in\mathsf{Asrt}
, contain Boolean assertions,
standard first-order connectives and quantifiers, and spatial
assertions.
Spatial assertions include: the empty memory assertion,
{\sf emp}
; the
positive cell assertion,
\mathit{E}_{1}\mapsto\mathit{E}_{2}
; the negative cell
assertion,
\mathit{E}\mapsto\varnothing
(as in
[
12
,
11
,
13
,
14
]
and denoted in ISL by

\mathit{E}\mathrel{{\mathclap{\not}{\mapsto}}}

[
27
]
), the separating conjunction (star); and its iteration (iterated star).

To define assertion satisfiability, we introduce
substitutions
,

\theta:\mathsf{LVar}\rightharpoonup_{\mathtt{fin}}\mathsf{Val}
, which are partial
finite mappings from logical variables to values,
extending expression evaluation of Definition
4
to

\llbracket{\mathit{E}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s}
straightforwardly, with a new base case for logical variables:

\llbracket{x}\rrbracket_{\theta,s}=\theta(x),\mbox{if $x\in{\sf dom}(\theta)$}\qquad\llbracket{x}\rrbracket_{\theta,s}=\lightning,\mbox{if $x\notin{\sf dom}(\theta)$}

Definition 7

(Satisfiability)
.

The assertion satisfiability relation, denoted by
\theta,\sigma\models P
, is defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{~}c@{~\ }l}\theta,(s,h)\models\pi&\Leftrightarrow&\llbracket{\pi}\rrbracket_{\theta,s}={\sf true}\land h=\emptyset\\
\theta,(s,h)\models\mathsf{False}&\Leftrightarrow&\text{never}\\
\theta,(s,h)\models P_{1}\Rightarrow P_{2}&\Leftrightarrow&\theta,(s,h)\models P_{1}\Rightarrow\theta,(s,h)\models P_{2}\\
\theta,(s,h)\models\exists\mathit{x}\ldotp P&\Leftrightarrow&\exists v\in\mathsf{Val}\ldotp\theta[\mathit{x}\mapsto v],(s,h)\models P\\
\theta,(s,h)\models{\sf emp}&\Leftrightarrow&{h=\emptyset}\\
\theta,(s,h)\models{\mathit{E}_{1}\mapsto\mathit{E}_{2}}&\Leftrightarrow&h=\{\llbracket{\mathit{E}_{1}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s}\mapsto\llbracket{\mathit{E}_{2}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s}\}\\
\theta,(s,h)\models{\mathit{E}_{1}\mapsto\varnothing}&\Leftrightarrow&h=\{\llbracket{\mathit{E}_{1}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s}\mapsto\varnothing\}\\
\theta,(s,h)\models P_{1}\mathrel{\star}P_{2}&\Leftrightarrow&\exists h_{1},h_{2}\ldotp h=h_{1}\uplus h_{2}\land\theta,(s,h_{1})\models P_{1}\land\theta,(s,h_{2})\models P_{2}\\
\theta,(s,h)\models\mathop{\mbox{\Large$\varoast$}}_{\mathit{E}_{1}\leq x<\mathit{E}_{2}}P&\Leftrightarrow&\exists h_{i},\ldots,h_{k-1}.\,h=\uplus_{j=i}^{k-1}h_{j}\land\forall j.\,i\leq j<k\Rightarrow\theta,(s,h_{j})\models P[j/x]\\
&&\text{where $i=\llbracket{\mathit{E}_{1}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s}$, $k=\llbracket{\mathit{E}_{2}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s}$, and $x$ is not free in $\mathit{E}_{1}$ or $\mathit{E}_{2}$}.\end{array}

Assertion satisfiability is defined in the standard way. For convenience, we choose Boolean assertions to be satisfiable only in the empty heap.

Definition 8

(Validity)
.

An assertion
P
is
valid
, denoted by
\models P
, iff
\forall\theta,\sigma.~\theta,\sigma\models P
.

4.2
Specifications

We define specifications for commands and functions, focussing in particular on external and internal function specifications and the relationship between them.

Definition 9
.

Specifications,
t={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}\right)}\in\mathcal{S}\mkern-1.0mupec
, comprise a
pre-condition
,
P
, a
success post-condition
,
Q_{\mathit{ok}}
, and a
faulting post-condition
,
Q_{\mathit{err}}
.

We denote that command
\mathsf{C}
has specification
t
by
\mathsf{C}:t
, or by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array}\right)}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}\right)}
in quadruple form. Additionally, we use the following shorthand:

\begin{array}[]{r@{~\defeq~}l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q\end{array})}~\triangleq&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array}\right)}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{False}\end{array}\right)}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q\end{array})}~\triangleq&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array}\right)}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{False}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q\end{array}\right)}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\mathcal{Q}}\end{array})}~\triangleq&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array}\right)}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}-\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}-\end{array}\right)}\end{array}

noting the use of
{\mathcal{Q}}
for cases in which the
post-condition details are not relevant.
We use quadruples rather than triples since, even though the post-condition could be expressed as a disjunction of
\mathit{ok}
- and
\mathit{err}
-labelled assertions, we find the quadruple distinction helpful as compound commands (e.g. sequence) treat the two differently (cf. Figure
3
).

The EX-validity of a specification
t
for a command
\mathsf{C}
in an implementation context
\gamma
requires both OX and UX frame-preserving validity.

Definition 10

(
\gamma
-Valid Specifications)
.

Given implementation context
\gamma
, command
\mathsf{C}
, and
specification
t={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}\right)}
,

t
is
\gamma
-valid for
\mathsf{C}
, denoted by
\gamma\models\mathsf{C}:t
or
\gamma\models{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array}\right)}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}\right)}
, if and only if:

\begin{array}[]{@{}l}\text{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}// Frame-preserving over-approximating validity }}\\
(\forall\theta,s,h,h_{f},o,s^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}.~\theta,(s,h)\models P\implies\\
\quad(s,h\uplus h_{f}),\mathsf{C}\Downarrow_{\gamma}o:(s^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime})\implies(o\neq\mathit{miss}\land\exists h^{\prime}.~h^{\prime\prime}=h^{\prime}\uplus h_{f}\land\theta,(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})\models Q_{o}))~\land\\
\text{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}// Frame-preserving under-approximating validity}}\\
(\forall\theta,s^{\prime},h^{\prime},h_{f},o.~\theta,(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})\models Q_{o}\implies h_{f}~\sharp~h^{\prime}\implies\\
\quad(\exists s,h\ldotp~\theta,(s,h)\models P~\land~(s,h\uplus h_{f}),\mathsf{C}\Downarrow_{\gamma}o:(s^{\prime},h^{\prime}\uplus h_{f})))\end{array}

Observe that the outcome
o
can either be success or a language error; it cannot be a missing resource error as this would break UX frame preservation.
As our operational semantics is complete, we can also use ESL
to characterise non-termination. In particular, if a
command satisfies a specification with both post-conditions

\mathsf{False}
, then the execution of the command is guaranteed to not terminate if executed from a
state satisfying the pre-condition. Were the semantics incomplete (for example, if it did not reason about errors), then such a
specification might also indicate the absence of a semantic
transition.

Compared to traditional OX reasoning, UX reasoning brings additional complexity to proofs of function specifications. To handle this complexity, we introduce two types of function specifications:
external
specifications, which provide the interface the function exposes to the client, and the related
internal
specifications, which provide the interface to the function implementation. This terminology is also used informally in InsecSL
[
24
]
. We use these in subsequent sections to show that ESL exhibits function compositionality.

Definition 11

(External Specifications)
.

A specification
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}\right)}
is
an external function specification if and only if:

•

P=(\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P^{\prime})
,
for some distinct program variables
\vec{\mathsf{x}}
, distinct logical variables
\vec{x}
, and assertion
P^{\prime}
, with
\mathtt{pv}(P^{\prime})=\emptyset
; and

•

either
\mathtt{pv}(Q_{\mathit{ok}})=\{\mathsf{ret}\}
or
Q_{\mathit{ok}}=\mathsf{False}
, and
either
\mathtt{pv}(Q_{\mathit{err}})=\{\mathsf{err}\}
or
Q_{\mathit{err}}=\mathsf{False}
.

The set of external specifications is denoted by
\mathcal{E\mkern-1.0muS}\mkern-1.0mupec
.

Definition 12

(Function Specification Contexts)
.

A function specification context,
\Gamma:\mathsf{Fid}\rightharpoonup_{\mathit{fin}}\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{E\mkern-1.0muS}\mkern-1.0mupec)
, is a finite partial function from function
identifiers to a set of external
specifications, with the more familiar notation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P\end{array}\right)}\ {f(\vec{\mathsf{x}})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}\right)}\in\Gamma
at times used in place of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}\right)}\in\Gamma(f)
.

The constraints on external
specifications are well-known from OX logics and follow the
usual scoping of function parameters and local variables, which are limited to the function body:
the pre-conditions
only contain the function
parameters,
\vec{\mathsf{x}}
; and the post-conditions may only have the
(dedicated) program variables
\mathsf{ret}
or
\mathsf{err}
, which hold,
respectively, the return value on successful termination or the
error value on faulting
termination.

Internal function specifications are more interesting for exact and UX than for OX
reasoning. The internal pre-condition is straightforward, extending the
external pre-condition by instantiating the local variables to
\mathtt{null}
. The
internal post-condition must therefore include information about the parameters
and local variables, as the internal specification cannot
lose information. This means that the connection between
internal and external post-conditions is subtle, given the constraints on the latter.
To address this, we define an internalisation function, relating
an external function specification with a set of possible internal
specifications. In particular, the external post-condition has to
be equivalent to an internal one in which the parameters and local
variables of the internal post-condition have been replaced by fresh
existentially quantified logical variables.

Definition 13

(Internalisation)
.

Given implementation context
\gamma
and function
f\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}(\gamma)
, a function specification internalisation,
\mathit{Int}_{\gamma,f}:\mathcal{E\mkern-1.0muS}\mkern-1.0mupec\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S}\mkern-1.0mupec)
, is defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{Int}_{\gamma,f}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}\right)})=\\
\quad\begin{array}[]{l@{\,\,}l}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\mathrel{\star}\vec{\mathsf{z}}=\mathtt{null}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}^{\prime}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}^{\prime}\end{array}\right)}\mid&\models Q_{\mathit{ok}}^{\prime}\Rightarrow\mathsf{E}\in\mathsf{Val}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{True}~\text{and}\\
&\models Q_{\mathit{ok}}\Leftrightarrow\exists\vec{p}\ldotp Q_{\mathit{ok}}^{\prime}[\vec{p}/\vec{\mathsf{p}}]\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=\mathsf{E}[\vec{p}/\vec{\mathsf{p}}]~\text{and}\\
&\models Q_{\mathit{err}}\Leftrightarrow\exists\vec{p}\ldotp Q_{\mathit{err}}^{\prime}[\vec{p}/\vec{\mathsf{p}}]\},\end{array}\end{array}

where
f(\vec{\mathsf{x}})\{\mathsf{C};\mathtt{return}\ \mathsf{E}\}\in\gamma
,
\vec{\mathsf{z}}=\mathtt{pv}(\mathsf{C})\setminus\mathtt{pv}(P)
,
\vec{\mathsf{p}}=\mathtt{pv}(P)\uplus\{\vec{\mathsf{z}}\}
, and the logical variables
\vec{p}
are fresh with respect to
Q_{\mathit{ok}}
and
Q_{\mathit{err}}
.

This approach also works for SL and ISL as well (with
\Leftarrow
instead of
\Leftrightarrow
for the post-conditions for SL, and
\Rightarrow
instead of
\Leftrightarrow
for ISL). It is not strictly necessary for SL, however, as information about program variables can
be forgotten in the internal post-conditions before the transition to the external post-condition.

Definition 14

(Environments)
.

An environment,
(\gamma,\Gamma)
, is a pair consisting of an
implementation context
\gamma
and a specification context

\Gamma
.

An environment
(\gamma,\Gamma)
is
valid

if and only if every function specified
in
\Gamma
has an implementation in
\gamma
and every specification in
\Gamma
has a
\gamma
-valid
internal specification.

Definition 15

(Valid Environments)
.

Given an implementation context
\gamma
and a
specification context
\Gamma
, the environment
(\gamma,\Gamma)

is valid, written
\models(\gamma,\Gamma)
, if and only if

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}(\Gamma)\subseteq\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}(\gamma)~\land\\
\quad(\forall f,\vec{\mathsf{x}},\mathsf{C},\mathsf{E}.~f(\vec{\mathsf{x}})\{\mathsf{C};\mathtt{return}\ \mathsf{E}\}\in\gamma\implies(\forall t.~t\in\Gamma(f)\implies\exists t^{\prime}\in\mathit{Int}_{\gamma,f}(t).~\gamma\models\mathsf{C}:t^{\prime}))\end{array}

Finally, a specification
t
is valid for a command
\mathsf{C}
in a
specification context
\Gamma

if and only if
t
is
\gamma
-valid for all implementation contexts

\gamma
that validate
\Gamma
.

Definition 16

(
\Gamma
-Valid Specifications)
.

Given a
specification context
\Gamma
, a command
\mathsf{C}
, and a specification

t={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\mathcal{Q}}\end{array})}
, the specification
t
is
\Gamma
-valid for
command
\mathsf{C}
, written
\Gamma\models\mathsf{C}:t
or
\Gamma\models{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\mathcal{Q}}\end{array})}
, if and only if

\forall\gamma\ldotp~\models(\gamma,\Gamma)\implies\gamma\models{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\mathcal{Q}}\end{array})}
.

4.3
Program Logic

We give the representative ESL proof rules in Figure
3
and all in Appendix
B
. We introduce and discuss in detail the function-related rules, given for the first time in a UX setting. We denote the repetition of the pre-condition in the post-condition by
\mathsf{pre}
.
When reading the rules, it is important to remember that we
must not drop paths and must not lose information
.
The judgement

\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array}\right)}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}\right)}
means that the specification
t
is
derivable
for a command
\mathsf{C}
given the specifications recorded in
\Gamma
.

The basic command rules are fairly straightforward. The

[nondet]
rule
existentially quantifies the generated value via

\mathsf{x}\mathbin{\in}\mathbb{N}
to capture all paths, in contrast with the
RHL
[
8
]
and ISL
[
27
]
rules,
which explicitly choose one value to describe one path. The

\mathit{E}^{\prime}\mathbin{\in}\mathsf{Val}
in the post-condition is necessary as we know that

\mathit{E}^{\prime}
evaluates to a value from the
pre-condition and cannot lose information;
the same principle applies to many other rules. The

[assign]
rule requires that the evaluation of
\mathsf{E}

does not fault in the pre-condition via
{\mathsf{E}\mathbin{\in}\mathsf{Val}}
.
Strictly speaking, we should
have an additional case in which the assigned variable is not in the store. To avoid
this clutter, we instead assume that program variables are always in
the store as we are analysing function bodies and, in our programming language, all local
variables are initialised on function entry. The error-related rules
capture cases in which expression evaluation faults
(e.g.
[lookup-err-val]
rule, using
\mathsf{E}\notin\mathsf{Val}
), expressions are of the
incorrect type, or memory is accessed after it has been freed (e.g.
[lookup-err-use-after-free]
rule, using

\mathsf{E}\mapsto\varnothing
). Note that missing
resource errors cannot be captured without breaking frame preservation, as the added-on
frame could contain the missing resource.

\frac{}{\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\sf emp}\end{array})}\ {\texttt{skip}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\sf emp}\end{array})}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{\mbox{skip}}\hskip 18.49988pt\frac{\mathsf{x}\notin\mathtt{pv}(\mathit{E}^{\prime})\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994ptQ\triangleq\mathit{E}^{\prime}\mathbin{\in}\mathsf{Val}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{x}\mathbin{\in}{\mathbb{N}}}{\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{x}=\mathit{E}^{\prime}\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{x}\mathtt{\ :=\ }\mathtt{nondet}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q\end{array})}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{\mbox{nondet}}\hskip 18.49988pt\frac{\mathsf{x}\notin\mathtt{pv}(\mathit{E}^{\prime})\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994ptQ\triangleq{\mathit{E}^{\prime}\mathbin{\in}\mathsf{Val}}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{x}=\mathsf{E}[\mathit{E}^{\prime}/\mathsf{x}]}{\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\mathsf{x}=\mathit{E}^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}{\mathsf{E}\mathbin{\in}\mathsf{Val}}}\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{x}\mathtt{\ :=\ }\mathsf{E}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q\end{array})}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{\mbox{assign}}\\
\frac{\mathsf{x}\notin\mathtt{pv}(\mathit{E}^{\prime})\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994ptQ\triangleq{\mathit{E}^{\prime}\mathbin{\in}\mathsf{Val}}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{x}=\mathit{E}_{1}[\mathit{E}^{\prime}/\mathsf{x}]\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{E}[\mathit{E}^{\prime}/\mathsf{x}]\mapsto\mathit{E}_{1}[\mathit{E}^{\prime}/\mathsf{x}]}{\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{x}=\mathit{E}^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{E}\mapsto\mathit{E}_{1}\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{x}\mathtt{\ :=\ }[\mathsf{E}]}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q\end{array})}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{\mbox{lookup}}\hskip 18.49988pt\frac{Q\triangleq\mbox{$\mathsf{E}_{1}$}\mapsto\mbox{$\mathsf{E}_{2}$}\mathrel{\star}\mathit{E}\mathbin{\in}\mathsf{Val}}{\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mbox{$\mathsf{E}_{1}$}\mapsto\mathit{E}\mathrel{\star}{\mathsf{E}_{2}\mathbin{\in}\mathsf{Val}}\end{array})}\ {[\mbox{$\mathsf{E}_{1}$}]\mathtt{\ :=\ }\mbox{$\mathsf{E}_{2}$}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q\end{array})}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{\mbox{mutate}}\\
\frac{\mathsf{x}\notin\mathtt{pv}(\mathit{E}^{\prime})\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994ptQ\triangleq\mathit{E}^{\prime}\mathbin{\in}\mathsf{Val}\mathrel{\star}\mathop{\mbox{\Large$\varoast$}}{{}_{0\leq i<\mathsf{E}[\mathit{E}^{\prime}/\mathsf{x}]}}((\mathsf{x}+i)\mapsto\mathtt{null})}{\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{x}=\mathit{E}^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{E}\mathbin{\in}\mathbb{N}\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{x}\mathtt{\ :=\ }\mathtt{new}(\mathsf{E})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(ok:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q\end{array})}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{\mbox{new}}\hskip 18.49988pt\frac{\mathit{E}_{\mathit{err}}\triangleq[``\mathsf{Error}",\mathsf{E}]}{\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\mathsf{E}\mathbin{\in}\mathsf{Val}}\end{array})}\ {\mathtt{error}(\mathsf{E})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{err}=\mathit{E}_{\mathit{err}}\end{array})}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{\mbox{error}}\\
\frac{Q\triangleq{\mathit{E}^{\prime}\mathbin{\in}\mathsf{Val}}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{E}\mapsto\varnothing}{\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{E}\mapsto\mathit{E}^{\prime}\end{array})}\ {\mathtt{free}(\mathsf{E})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(ok:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q\end{array})}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{\mbox{free}}\hskip 18.49988pt\frac{P\triangleq\mathsf{x}=\mathit{E}^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}E\mathbin{\notin}\mathsf{Val}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\mathit{E}_{\mathit{err}}\triangleq[``\mathsf{ExprEval}",\mathsf{str}(\mathsf{E})]}{\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{x}\mathtt{\ :=\ }[\mathsf{E}]}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}^{*}\end{array})}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{\mbox{lookup-err-val}}\hskip 18.49988pt\frac{P\triangleq\mathsf{x}=\mathit{E}^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{E}\mapsto\varnothing\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\mathit{E}_{\mathit{err}}\triangleq[``\mathsf{UseAfterFree}",\mathsf{str}(\mathsf{E}),\mathsf{E}]}{\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{x}\mathtt{\ :=\ }[\mathsf{E}]}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}^{*}\end{array})}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{\mbox{lookup-err-use-after-free}}\\
\frac{\mathsf{C}\triangleq\mathtt{if}\ (\mathsf{E})\ \mathsf{C}_{1}\ \mathtt{else}\ \mathsf{C}_{2}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{E}\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{C}_{1}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\mathcal{Q}}\end{array})}}{\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{E}\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\mathcal{Q}}\end{array})}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{\mbox{if-then}}\hskip 18.49988pt\frac{\mathsf{C}\triangleq\mathtt{if}\ (\mathsf{E})\ \mathsf{C}_{1}\ \mathtt{else}\ \mathsf{C}_{2}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\mathrel{\star}\lnot\mathsf{E}\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{C}_{2}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\mathcal{Q}}\end{array})}}{\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\mathrel{\star}\lnot\mathsf{E}\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\mathcal{Q}}\end{array})}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{\mbox{if-else}}\hskip 18.49988pt\frac{\mathsf{C}\triangleq\mathtt{if}\ (\mathsf{E})\ \mathsf{C}_{1}\ \mathtt{else}\ \mathsf{C}_{2}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\mathit{E}_{\mathit{err}}\triangleq["\mathsf{ExprEval}",\mathsf{str}(\mathsf{E})]}{\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{E}\mathbin{\notin}\mathsf{Val}\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}^{*}\end{array})}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{\mbox{if-err-val}}\\
\frac{{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{C}_{1}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}R\end{array})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}^{1}\end{array})}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}R\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{C}_{2}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}^{2}\end{array})}\end{array}}}{\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{C}_{1};\ \mathsf{C}_{2}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}^{1}\lor Q_{\mathit{err}}^{2}\end{array})}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{\mbox{seq}}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\forall i\in\mathbb{N}\ldotp~\models P_{i}\Rightarrow\mathsf{E}\in\mathbb{B}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{True}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\forall i\in\mathbb{N}\ldotp~\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P_{i}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{E}\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P_{i+1}\end{array})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{i}\end{array})}}{\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P_{0}\end{array})}\ {\mathtt{while}\ (\mathsf{E})\ \mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\neg\mathsf{E}\mathrel{\star}\exists i\ldotp P_{i}\end{array})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\exists i\ldotp Q_{i}\end{array})}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{\mbox{while}}\hskip 9.24994pt\\
\frac{{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P^{\prime}\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}^{\prime}\end{array})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}^{\prime}\end{array})}\hskip 9.24994pt\models P^{\prime},Q_{\mathit{ok}}^{\prime},Q_{\mathit{err}}^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow P,Q_{\mathit{ok}},Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}}}{\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array})}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{\mbox{equiv}}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathtt{mod}(\mathsf{C})\cap\mathtt{fv}(R)=\emptyset\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array})}\end{array}}}{\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\mathrel{\star}R\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\mathrel{\star}R\end{array})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\mathrel{\star}R\end{array})}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{\mbox{frame}}\\
\frac{\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array})}}{\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\exists x.\,P\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\exists x\ldotp Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\exists x\ldotp Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array})}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{\mbox{exists}}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P_{1}\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}^{1}\end{array})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}^{1}\end{array})}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P_{2}\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}^{2}\end{array})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}^{2}\end{array})}}{\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P_{1}\lor P_{2}\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}^{1}\lor Q_{\mathit{ok}}^{2}\end{array})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}^{1}\lor Q_{\mathit{err}}^{2}\end{array})}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{\mbox{disj}}

Figure 3:
ESL proof rules (excerpt), with
Q_{\mathit{err}}^{*}=(\mathsf{pre}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{err}=\mathit{E}_{\mathit{err}})

When it comes to composite commands, we opt for two

\mathtt{if}
-rules, covering the branches separately.
The sequencing rule shows how exact quadruples of successive
commands can be joined together, highlighting, in particular, how
errors are collected using disjunction. One interesting aspect of this rule is
what happens when
C_{1}
only throws an error or does not terminate, meaning that
R=\mathsf{False}
.
In both those cases, given the exactness of the rules, it has to be that
Q_{\mathit{ok}}=Q_{\mathit{err}}^{2}=\mathsf{False}
,
and the post-condition of the sequence becomes
(\mathit{ok}:\mathsf{False})\ (\mathit{err}:Q_{\mathit{err}}^{1}\lor~\mathsf{False})
,
meaning that, if
C_{1}
only throws an error (that is,
Q_{\mathit{err}}^{1}\neq\mathsf{False}
) then that is
the only error that can come out of the sequence, and if
C_{1}
does not terminate (that is,
Q_{\mathit{err}}^{1}=\mathsf{False}
)
then the sequence does not terminate either.

The while rule is an adaptation of the RHL while rule
[
8
]
, generalising the invariant of the SL while rule with two natural-number-indexed families of variants,
P_{i}
and
Q_{i}
, which explicitly maintain the iteration index. Note how the
i
in the premise is a meta-variable representing a natural number, which in the conclusion gets substituted for an existentially quantified logical variable; a similar principle will be applied later when dealing with environment extension. Interestingly, this rule does not require adjustment to reason about non-termination.

The structural rules are not surprising, with equivalence
replacing the forward/backward consequence of OX/UX reasoning
and with frame, existential
introduction, and disjunction affecting both post-conditions.
Disjunction allows us to derive the standard SL
\mathtt{if}
rule, which captures both branches at the same time.
Note that there, however, is no sound conjunction rule,
as the conjunction rules of SL and ISL cannot be combined in
ESL, since conjunction does not distribute over the star in both
directions, breaking frame preservation.

Function Call.

We discuss the ESL function-call rule in detail, creating it starting from the standard OX-sound SL rule, adapted for quadruples:

\small\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P\end{array}\right\}}\ {f(\vec{\mathsf{x}})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left\{ok:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array}\right\}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left\{err:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}\right\}}\in\Gamma\hskip 18.49988pt\mathsf{y}\not\in\mathtt{pv}(\mathit{E}_{y})}{\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{y}=\mathit{E}_{y}\mathrel{\star}\vec{\mathsf{E}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P\end{array}\right\}}\ {\mathsf{y}\mathtt{\ :=\ }f(\vec{\mathsf{E}})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left\{ok:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}[\mathsf{y}/\mathtt{ret}]\end{array}\right\}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left\{err:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{y}=\mathit{E}_{y}\mathrel{\star}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}\right\}}}

In order to make this rule UX-sound, we only have to ensure that no information from the pre-condition is lost in the post-conditions. In particular, we have to remember:

•

that the evaluation of
\mathit{E}_{y}
does not fault, captured by
\mathit{E}_{y}\in\mathsf{Val}
and needed in the success post-condition only, as it is already implied by the error post-condition; and

•

that
\vec{\mathsf{E}}=\vec{x}
holds (with the substitution
[\mathit{E}_{y}/\mathsf{y}]
needed in the success post-condition as the value of
\mathsf{y}
may change if the function call succeeds);

bringing us to the ESL function call rule:

\small\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P\end{array}\right)}\ {f(\vec{\mathsf{x}})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}\right)}\in\Gamma\hskip 18.49988pt\mathsf{y}\not\in\mathtt{pv}(\mathit{E}_{y})\hskip 9.24994ptQ_{\mathit{ok}}^{\prime}\triangleq\mathit{E}_{y}\mathbin{\in}\mathsf{Val}\mathrel{\star}\vec{\mathsf{E}}[\mathit{E}_{y}/\mathsf{y}]=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}Q_{\mathit{ok}}[\mathsf{y}/\mathtt{ret}]\hskip 18.49988ptQ_{\mathit{err}}^{\prime}\triangleq\mathsf{y}=\mathit{E}_{y}\mathrel{\star}\vec{\mathsf{E}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}Q_{\mathit{err}}}{\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{y}=\mathit{E}_{y}\mathrel{\star}\vec{\mathsf{E}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P\end{array}\right)}\ {\mathsf{y}\mathtt{\ :=\ }f(\vec{\mathsf{E}})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}^{\prime}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}^{\prime}\end{array}\right)}}

Environment Formation.

Whereas the ESL function-call rule does not deviate substantially from its OX counterpart, the environment formation rules illustrate the difference in complexity between OX and UX function compositionality. These rules use the judgement
\vdash(\gamma,\Gamma)
to state that the environment
(\gamma,\Gamma)
is
well-formed
. The base case,
\vdash(\emptyset,\emptyset)
, is trivial and the same as for SL, stating that the environment consisting of an empty implementation context and an empty specification context is well-formed. For illustrative purposes, we give a simplified version of the extension rule, extending the environment with a single, possibly recursive, function. The full rule, which extends the environment with a group of mutually recursive functions, is given in Appendix
B
.
We start from the corresponding OX-sound rule from SL:

\small\frac{{\begin{array}[]{c}\vdash(\gamma,\Gamma)\hskip 9.24994ptf\not\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}(\gamma)\hskip 9.24994pt\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma[f\mapsto(\vec{\mathsf{x}},\mathsf{C},\mathsf{E})]\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma[f\mapsto\{t\}]\hskip 9.24994pt\exists t^{\prime}\in\mathit{Int}_{\gamma^{\prime},f}(t).~\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathsf{C}:t^{\prime}\end{array}}}{\vdash(\gamma^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime})}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{env-extend-sl}

which states that a well-formed environment
(\gamma,\Gamma)
can be extended with a given function
f
and its external specification
t
to
(\gamma^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime})
if some corresponding internal specification of
f
can be proven for the body of
f
under the extended specification context
\Gamma^{\prime}
.
2
2

2

Internalisation is normally omitted in SL as forward consequence allows information about program variables to be lost, making internal and external post-conditions of SL specifications almost the same.
Note that using
\Gamma^{\prime}
means that a specification can be used to prove itself, which is sound in SL but unsound in ISL: specifically, it would allow us to prove UX-invalid specifications of non-terminating functions. For example, we
would be able to prove that the function

\mathtt{f}()~\{~\mathsf{r}:=\mathtt{f}();~\mathtt{return}\ \mathsf{r}~\}

satisfies the EX-valid specification

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\sf emp}\end{array})}\ {\mathtt{f}()}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{False}\end{array})}
, but also the
EX-invalid specification
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\sf emp}\end{array})}\ {\mathtt{f}()}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{ret}=42\end{array})}
. The latter is vacuously OX-valid as there are
no terminating executions, but when considered from the UX viewpoint,
it implies the existence of an execution path from the pre- to the
post-condition, contradicting the non-termination of
\mathtt{f}
.

Therefore, to be soundly usable in UX reasoning, specifications with satisfiable post-conditions (onward: terminating specifications) must come with a mechanism that disallows the above counter-example. We achieve this by following a standard approach for reasoning about termination
[
9
,
7
]
, based on decreasing measures on well-ordered sets.
In particular, we require the terminating specification to be proven,
t\triangleq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}\right)}
to be extended with a
measure

\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
, denoting this extension by
t(\alpha)
:
3
3

3

We can extend the measure beyond natural numbers to computable ordinals,
\mathcal{O}\triangleq\omega_{1}^{\text{CK}}
