, allowing us to reason about a broader set of functions, such as those with non-deterministic nested recursion (cf. Appendix
B
).

\begin{array}[]{r@{~\defeq~}l}t(\alpha)~\triangleq&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\mathrel{\star}\alpha=E_{\mu}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\mathrel{\star}\alpha=E_{\mu}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\mathrel{\star}\alpha=E_{\mu}\end{array}\right)}\end{array}

where
E_{\mu}
is a logical expression describing how the measure is computed.
Then, we prove that
t(\alpha)
holds for every specific
\alpha
, assuming that recursive calls to
f
can only use the terminating specifications
t(\beta)
of a measure
\beta

strictly smaller
than
\alpha
.
This restriction is standard and, if the proof succeeds, ensures that
f
has at least one terminating execution. Also, it disallows the above-mentioned counter-example, as no measure given in the pre-condition would be able to decrease before the recursive call. Importantly, the measure is only a tool required for proving specification validity and once this proof has been completed, the specification without the measure is added to
\Gamma
and can be used in proofs of client code.

In addition, we incorporate reasoning about non-terminating specifications (NT-specifications). This is relevant in situations in which the operational semantics of the analysed language is complete, which allows non-termination to be captured using the post-condition
\mathsf{False}
. As NT-specifications are vacuously UX-sound, our focus is on ensuring their OX-soundness, which we do by again imposing a measure
\alpha
, but allowing recursive calls for a measure
\beta

smaller or equal
than
\alpha
, that is, for an NT-specification to be used to prove itself. This, for example, allows for a proof of the specification
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\sf emp}\end{array})}\ {\mathtt{f}()~\{~\mathsf{r}:=\mathtt{f}();~\mathtt{return}\ \mathsf{r}~\}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{False}\end{array})}
by choosing a constant measure
\alpha
. The ESL environment extension rule, therefore, is as follows:

\small\frac{{\begin{array}[]{l}\text{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}// Assume valid environment, extend implementation context with new function $f$ }}\hskip 9.24994pt\vdash(\gamma,\Gamma)\hskip 18.49988ptf\not\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}(\gamma)\hskip 18.49988pt\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma[f\mapsto(\vec{\mathsf{x}},\mathsf{C},\mathsf{E})]\hskip 9.24994pt\text{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}// Extend the specifications of $f$ with a measure $\alpha$}}\hskip 9.24994ptt\triangleq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}\right)}\hskip 9.24994ptt_{\infty}\triangleq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P_{\infty}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{False}\end{array}\right)}\hskip 9.24994ptt_{\infty}(\alpha)\triangleq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P_{\infty}\mathrel{\star}\alpha=E_{\mu}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{False}\end{array}\right)}\hskip 9.24994ptt(\alpha)\triangleq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\mathrel{\star}\alpha=E_{\mu}\end{array}\right)}\ {\!}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\mathrel{\star}\alpha=E_{\mu}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\mathrel{\star}\alpha=E_{\mu}\end{array}\right)}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt~\hskip 9.24994pt\text{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}// Construct $\Gamma(\alpha)$: assume $t$ for measure $\beta<\alpha$ {and $t_{\infty}$ for measure $\beta\leq\alpha$}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma(\alpha)=\Gamma[f\mapsto\{t(\beta)\mid{\beta<\alpha}\}\mathbin{\cup}\{t_{\infty}\mid\beta\leq\alpha\}]\hskip 9.24994pt\text{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}// For every $\alpha$, prove internal specifications of $f$ corresponding to $t$ {and $t_{\infty}$}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\forall\alpha\ldotp\exists t^{\prime}\in\mathit{Int}_{\gamma^{\prime},f}\big(t(\alpha)\big).~\Gamma(\alpha)\vdash\mathsf{C}:t^{\prime}\hskip 18.49988pt{\forall\alpha\ldotp\exists t^{\prime}\in\mathit{Int}_{\gamma^{\prime},f}(t_{\infty}(\alpha)).~\Gamma(\alpha)\vdash\mathsf{C}:t^{\prime}}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}// Extend $\Gamma$ with $t$ {and $t_{\infty}$}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma^{\prime}:=\Gamma[f\mapsto\{t,t_{\infty}\}]\end{array}}}{\vdash(\gamma^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime})}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{\mbox{env-extend}}

4.4
Soundness

We state the soundness results for ESL and give intuition about the proofs; the full proofs can be found in Appendix
C
,
D
, and
E
.

Theorem 17
.

Any derivable specification is valid:

\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\mathcal{Q}}\end{array})}\implies\Gamma\models{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\mathcal{Q}}\end{array})}
.

Proof.

By induction on
\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\mathcal{Q}}\end{array})}
. Most cases are straightforward; the
[fun-call]
rule obtains a valid specification for the function body from the validity of the environment.
∎

Theorem 18
.

Any well-formed environment is valid:

\vdash(\gamma,\Gamma)\implies\;\models(\gamma,\Gamma)
.

Proof.

At the core of the proof is a lemma stating that
\models(\gamma,\Gamma)\implies(\forall\alpha\ldotp\models(\gamma^{\prime},\Gamma(\alpha)))
, where
\gamma^{\prime}
and
\Gamma(\alpha)
have been obtained from
\gamma
and
\Gamma
as per
[env-extend]
. Using this lemma, we derive the desired
\models(\gamma^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime})
, where
\Gamma^{\prime}
is obtained from
\Gamma
and
\Gamma(\alpha)
as per
[env-extend]
. The proof of this lemma is done by transfinite induction on
\alpha
and has the standard zero, successor, and limit ordinal cases. We outline the proof for the case in which a single, possibly recursive, function
f
with body
\mathsf{C}_{f}
is added; the generalisation to
n
mutually recursive functions is straightforward and can be seen in Appendix
E
.

In all three cases, the soundness of all specifications except the NT-specification with the highest considered ordinal follows from the inductive hypothesis. This remaining NT-specification is vacuously UX-valid, meaning that we only need to prove its OX-validity.
For this, we use a form of fixpoint induction called Scott induction (see, e.g., Winskel
[
30
]
), required when specifications can be used to prove themselves (e.g. any SL specification).

We set up the Scott induction by extending the set of commands with two pseudo-commands,
\mathtt{scope}
and
\mathtt{choice}
, with the former modelling the function call but allowing arbitrary commands to be executed in place of the function body, and the latter denoting non-deterministic choice.
We then construct the greatest-fixpoint closure of these extended commands, denoted by
\mathbb{C}
, whose elements may contain infinite applications of the command constructors. We define a behavioural equivalence relation
\simeq_{\gamma^{\prime}}
on
\mathbb{C}
and denote by
\mathbb{C}_{\gamma^{\prime}}
the obtained quotient space. This relation induces a partial order
\sqsubseteq_{\gamma^{\prime}}
, and a join operator that coincides with
\mathtt{choice}
, and we show that
(\mathbb{C}_{\gamma^{\prime}},\sqsubseteq_{\gamma^{\prime}})
is a domain.

We next define
S^{\alpha}
as the set of all equivalence classes that hold an element that, for every specification in
(\Gamma(\alpha))(f)
, OX-satisfies at least one of its internal specifications, and show that
S^{\alpha}
is an admissible subset of
\mathbb{C}_{\gamma^{\prime}}
, that is, that it contains the least element of
\mathbb{C}_{\gamma^{\prime}}
(represented, for example, by the infinite loop
\mathtt{while}~({\sf true})~\{~\mathtt{skip}~\}
) and is chain-closed.

We then define the function
h(\mathsf{C})\triangleq\mathsf{C}_{f}[\mathsf{C},\gamma^{\prime},f]
, which replaces
all function calls to
f
in
\mathsf{C}_{f}
with
\mathsf{C}
using the
\mathtt{scope}
command, and the function
g
as the lifting of
h
to
\mathbb{C}_{\gamma^{\prime}}
:

g([\mathsf{C}]):=\left[h(\mathsf{C})\right]
.
We next prove that
g
is continuous (that is, monotonic and supremum-preserving) and that
g(S^{\alpha})\subseteq S^{\alpha}
, from which we can apply the Scott induction principle, together with a well-known identity of the least-fixpoint, which implies that
\mathsf{C}_{f}\in\text{\it lfp}(g)
, to obtain that
[\mathsf{C}_{f}]\in S^{\alpha}
. From there, we are finally able to prove that
\models(\gamma^{\prime},\Gamma(\alpha))
.
∎

These two theorems, to the best of our knowledge, are the first to demonstrate sound function compositionality for UX logics. Previous work on UX logics
[
27
,
28
,
24
]
used function specifications in examples but did not include rules in the logic for calling functions and managing a function specification environment. Program logics that do not include function rules and function specification environments in effect delegate soundness responsibilities to the meta-logic within which they are embedded. This might be appropriate in some contexts, such as in interactive theorem provers, whose meta-logic is reliable. Charguéraud’s clean-slate tutorial SL implementation in Coq
[
6
]
, for example, does not provide either function call rules or program-logic-level infrastructure for a function specification environment; instead, it relies on Coq’s induction mechanism and definitional mechanism to use and store function specifications. However, when implementing an SL/ISL/ESL-based tool in a mainstream non-ITP language, such as C++ or OCaml, no reliable meta-logic that can act as a safety net is available. This is particularly concerning for UX logics, which require complex rules for handling functions, including forgetting information about function-local mutable variables. In ESL, the handling of functions is fully internalised into the logic, no meta-logic facilities are required to handle function calls, and the program-logic-level facilities of ESL for handling functions are validated by the soundness proof.

The proof of Theorem
18
can be adjusted for ISL: the function call rule would remain the same, and
[env-extend]
would not include NT-specifications, removing the need for Scott induction. On the other hand, the Scott induction itself could be easily adapted for SL.

5
Examples: ESL in Practice

We demonstrate how to use ESL to specify and verify correctness and incorrectness properties of data-structure algorithms, focussing on singly-linked lists and binary search trees.

We investigate, for the first time, the use of abstract predicates in a UX program logic, decoupling abstraction from over-approximation. Our findings show that UX/EX specifications can soundly incorporate abstraction, but also that it, ultimately, cannot be used as freely as in the OX setting. Firstly, since UX reasoning cannot lose information, not all algorithms can be UX-specified at all levels of abstraction, and hence sometimes specifications have to be less abstract than in OX reasoning. Secondly, because specifications are only composable when expressed at the same level of abstraction, specifications of a library client have to be written at the “least common level of abstraction” of the specifications of all of the library functions that the client calls.

Building on §
2
, we give further intuition on how to think informally about UX/EX specifications using a number of list algorithms and predicates describing lists with various degrees of abstraction (§
5.1
, §
5.2
), more detail on how to write formal ESL proofs (§
5.3
), and examples of ESL reasoning for binary-search-tree algorithms (§
5.4
).

5.1
List Predicates

We implement singly-linked lists in the standard way: every list consists of two contiguous cells in the heap (denoted
x\mapsto a,b
, meaning
x\mapsto a\mathrel{\star}x+1\mapsto b
), with the first holding the value of the node, the second holding a pointer to the next node in the list, and the list terminating with a
\mathtt{null}
pointer.
To capture lists in ESL, we use a number of list predicates:

\begin{array}[]{r@{~}c@{~}l}\mathsf{list}(x)&\triangleq&(x=\mathtt{null})~\lor(\exists v,x^{\prime}\ldotp x\mapsto v,x^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x^{\prime}))\\[2.84526pt]
\mathsf{list}(x,n)&\triangleq&(x=\mathtt{null}\mathrel{\star}n=0)\lor(\exists v,x^{\prime}\ldotp x\mapsto v,x^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x^{\prime},n-1))\\[2.84526pt]
\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{vs})&\triangleq&(x=\mathtt{null}\mathrel{\star}\mathit{vs}=[~])\lor(\exists v,x^{\prime},\mathit{vs}^{\prime}\ldotp x\mapsto v,x^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x^{\prime},\mathit{vs}^{\prime})\mathrel{\star}\mathit{vs}=v:\mathit{vs}^{\prime})\\[2.84526pt]
\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs})&\triangleq&(x=\mathtt{null}\mathrel{\star}\mathit{xs}=[~])\lor(\exists v,x^{\prime},\mathit{xs}^{\prime}\ldotp x\mapsto v,x^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x^{\prime},\mathit{xs}^{\prime})\mathrel{\star}\mathit{xs}=x:\mathit{xs}^{\prime})\\[2.84526pt]
\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs},\mathit{vs})&\triangleq&(x=\mathtt{null}\mathrel{\star}\mathit{xs}=[~]\mathrel{\star}\mathit{vs}=[~])~\lor\\[0.7113pt]
&&(\exists v,x^{\prime},\mathit{xs}^{\prime},\mathit{vs}^{\prime}\ldotp x\mapsto v,x^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x^{\prime},\mathit{xs}^{\prime},\mathit{vs}^{\prime})\mathrel{\star}\mathit{xs}=x:\mathit{xs}^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}\mathit{vs}=v:\mathit{vs}^{\prime})\\[2.84526pt]
\end{array}

These predicates expose different parts of the list
structure in their parameters,
hiding
the rest via
existential quantification: the

\mathsf{list}(x)
predicate hides all information about the represented mathematical list,
just declaring that there is a singly-linked list at address
x
;
the

\mathsf{list}(x,n)
predicate hides the internal node addresses and values,
exposing the list length via the parameter
n
;
the
\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs})
predicate hides information about the values
of the mathematical list, exposing the internal addresses of the list
via the parameter
\mathit{xs}
;
the
\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{vs})
predicate hides information about the internal
addresses, exposing the list’s values via the parameter
\mathit{vs}
;
and the list predicate
\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs},\mathit{vs})
hides nothing, exposing the entire node-value structure via the parameters
\mathit{xs}
and
\mathit{vs}
.
These predicates are related to each other via logical equivalence; for example, it holds that:

\begin{array}[]{c@{\qquad}c}\models\mathsf{list}(x)\Leftrightarrow\exists n.~\mathsf{list}(x,n)&\models\mathsf{list}(x)\Leftrightarrow\exists\mathit{vs}.~\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{vs})\\[2.84526pt]
\models\mathsf{list}(x)\Leftrightarrow\exists\mathit{xs},\mathit{vs}.~\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs},\mathit{vs})&\models\mathsf{list}(x,n)\Leftrightarrow\exists\mathit{vs}.~\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{vs})\mathrel{\star}|\mathit{vs}|=n\end{array}

5.2
Writing UX/EX Abstract Specifications

We consider a number of list algorithms, described in words, and guide the reader on how to write correct UX/EX specifications for these algorithms using the list abstractions given in the previous section (§
5.1
), comparing how the UX/EX approach and specifications differ from their OX counterparts. We provide detailed proofs and implementations for each type of algorithm (iterative/recursive, allocating/deallocating, pure/mutative, etc.) in Appendix
F
.

An important point is to understand how to look for counter-examples to a given specification: from the definition of OX validity, it follows that breaking OX reasoning amounts to “finding a state in the pre-condition (pre-model) for which the execution of
f
terminates and does not end in a state in the post-condition”; from the definition of UX validity, breaking UX reasoning means “finding a model of the post-condition (post-model) not reachable by execution of
f
from any state in the pre-condition”; and from the definition of EX validity, it follows that breaking EX reasoning means breaking either OX or UX reasoning. In addition, it is useful to remember that, for breaking UX validity, it is sufficient to find information known in the pre-condition but lost in the post-condition.

Length.

We first revisit the list-length function
\mathtt{LLen}(\mathsf{x})
, which takes a list at address
\mathsf{x}
, does not modify it, and returns its length. In §
2
, we have shown that it satisfies the exact specification

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,n)\end{array})}\ {~\mathtt{LLen}(\mathsf{x})~}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{list}(x,n)\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=n\end{array})}

and observed that

(O1)
abstraction does not always equal over-approximation.

Using similar reasoning, we can come to the conclusion that the following, less abstract specifications for
\mathtt{LLen}(\mathsf{x})
are also EX-valid:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{vs})\end{array})}\ {~\mathtt{LLen}(\mathsf{x})~}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{vs})\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=|\mathit{vs}|\end{array})}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs},\mathit{vs})\end{array})}\ {~\mathtt{LLen}(\mathsf{x})~}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs},\mathit{vs})\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=|\mathit{xs}|\end{array})}\end{array}

On the other hand, if we consider the following OX-valid specification:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x)\end{array}\right\}}\ {~\mathtt{LLen}(\mathsf{x})~}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\exists n\in\mathbb{N}.~\mathsf{list}(x)\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=n\end{array}\right\}}

we see that it is not UX-valid as the post-condition does not connect the return value to the list.
In particular, if we choose a post-model for
\mathsf{list}(x)
that has length 2,
but then choose, for example,
\mathsf{ret}=42
, we run into a problem: as the algorithm
does not modify the list, we have to choose the same model of
\mathsf{list}(x)
for the pre-model to have a chance of reaching the post-model, but then the algorithm will return 2, not 42, meaning that this specification is indeed not UX/EX-valid. From this discussion, we observe that:

(O2)
in valid UX/EX specifications, data-structure abstractions used in a post-condition must expose enough information to capture the behaviour of the function being specified with respect to the information given in the pre-condition.

Note that, given a specification less abstract than strictly needed, one can obtain more abstract ones by using validity-preserving transformations on specifications that correspond to the structural rules of the logic. We refer to these as
admissible
transformations, give the ones for existential introduction and equivalence below, and the rest in Appendix
B
:

\small\frac{\Gamma\models{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P\end{array}\right)}\ {f(\vec{\mathsf{x}})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}\right)}\hskip 18.49988pty\notin\vec{x}}{\Gamma\models{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}\exists y.~P\end{array}\right)}\ {f(\vec{\mathsf{x}})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\exists y.~Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathit{err}:\exists y.~Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}\right)}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{\mbox{adm-exists}}\frac{{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\models{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P^{\prime}\end{array}\right)}\ {f(\vec{\mathsf{x}})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}^{\prime}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}^{\prime}\end{array}\right)}\hskip 18.49988pt\models P^{\prime},Q_{\mathit{ok}}^{\prime},Q_{\mathit{err}}^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow P,Q_{\mathit{ok}},Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}}}{\Gamma\models{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P\end{array}\right)}\ {f(\vec{\mathsf{x}})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}\right)}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{\mbox{adm-equiv}}\par

For list-length, starting from the least abstract specification using
\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs},\mathit{vs})
, we can derive, for example, the specification using
\mathsf{list}(x,n)
, as follows:

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\quad}l}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs},\mathit{vs})\end{array})}\ {~\mathtt{LLen}(\mathsf{x})~}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs},\mathit{vs})\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=|\mathit{xs}|\end{array})}\\
\text{{{[adm-exists]}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\exists\mathit{vs}.~\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs},\mathit{vs})\end{array})}\ {~\mathtt{LLen}(\mathsf{x})~}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\exists\mathit{vs}.~\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs},\mathit{vs})\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=|\mathit{xs}|\end{array})}\\
\text{{{[adm-exists]}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\exists\mathit{xs},\mathit{vs}.~\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs},\mathit{vs})\end{array})}\ {~\mathtt{LLen}(\mathsf{x})~}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\exists\mathit{xs},\mathit{vs}.~\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs},\mathit{vs})\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=|\mathit{xs}|\end{array})}\\
\text{{{[adm-equiv]}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,n)\end{array})}\ {~\mathtt{LLen}(\mathsf{x})~}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{list}(x,n)\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=n\end{array})}\end{array}

Interestingly, from one more application of
[adm-exists]
and
[adm-equiv]
, we can derive

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x)\end{array})}\ {~\mathtt{LLen}(\mathsf{x})~}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\exists n.~\mathsf{list}(x,n)\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=n\end{array})}

which further illustrates observation (O2), in that even though the pre-condition does not talk about the length of the list, the post-condition has to expose it because the function output depends on it, and hence the post-condition must connect up the return value to the length of the list, here by an existentially quantified variable.

This approach of deriving abstract specifications can be used in general for working with ESL: for a given algorithm, first prove the least abstract specification, which exposes all details, and then adjust the degree of abstraction to fit the needs of the client code. We discuss this further in the upcoming paragraph on reasoning about client programs.

Membership.

Next, we consider the list-membership function
\mathtt{LMem}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{v})
, which takes a list at address
\mathsf{x}
, does not modify it, and returns
{\sf true}
if
\mathsf{v}
is in the list, and false otherwise. Given (O2) and the fact that the function output depends on the values in the list, we understand that, for its UX/EX specification, we should be using a list abstraction that exposes at least the values, that is,
\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{vs})
or
\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs},\mathit{vs})
. The corresponding specifications are:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{v}=v\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{vs})\end{array})}\ {~\mathtt{LMem}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{v})~}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{vs})\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=(v\in\mathit{vs})\end{array})}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{v}=v\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs},\mathit{vs})\end{array})}\ {~\mathtt{LMem}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{v})~}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs},\mathit{vs})\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=(v\in\mathit{vs})\end{array})}\end{array}

and are proven similarly to list-length. We can check that a more abstract specification, say:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{v}=v\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x)\end{array}\right\}}\ {~\mathtt{LMem}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{v})~}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\exists b\in\mathbb{B}.~\mathsf{list}(x)\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=b\end{array}\right\}}

is not UX-valid, by choosing, as the post-model,
b
to be false and the list at
x
to contain
v
. As for list-length, since list-membership does not modify the list, we have to choose the same model of
\mathsf{list}(x)
for the pre-model to have a chance of reaching the post-model, but then the algorithm will return
{\sf true}
, not
{\sf false}
, so this post-model is not reachable.

Swap-First-Two.

Next, we consider the list-swap-first-two function
\mathtt{LSwapFirstTwo}(\mathsf{x})
, which takes a list at address
\mathsf{x}
, swaps its first two values if the list is of sufficient length, returning
\mathtt{null}
, and throws an error otherwise without modifying the list. Given (O2), to specify this function we need an abstraction that captures list length and, apparently, also the list values; for example,
\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{vs})
. As this function can throw errors, its full EX specification has to use the ESL quadruple, in which the two post-conditions are constrained with the corresponding, shaded, branching conditions:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{vs}))}\\
{\mathtt{LSwapFirstTwo}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{v})}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{ok}:\exists v_{1},v_{2},\mathit{vs}^{\prime}.~\hbox{\pagecolor{slgray}$\mathit{vs}=v_{1}:v_{2}:\mathit{vs}^{\prime}$}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,v_{2}:v_{1}:\mathit{vs}^{\prime})\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=\mathtt{null})}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{err}:\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{vs})\mathrel{\star}\hbox{\pagecolor{slgray}$|\mathit{vs}|<2$}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{err}=\mathtt{``List~too~short!"})}\end{array}

observing that the success post-condition, given the used abstraction, has to not only state that the length of the list is not less than two, but also how the values are manipulated, and also that the error message is chosen for illustrative purposes.

However, note that the swapped values
are not featured in the function output
, but instead remain contained within the predicate. This indicates that a more abstract specification:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,n))}\\
{\mathtt{LSwapFirstTwo}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{v})}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{ok}:\mathsf{list}(x,n)\mathrel{\star}\hbox{\pagecolor{slgray}$n\geq 2$}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=\mathtt{null})}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{err}:\mathsf{list}(x,n)\mathrel{\star}\hbox{\pagecolor{slgray}$n<2$}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{err}=\mathtt{``List~too~short!"})}\end{array}

which only reveals the list length, might be EX-valid, and indeed it is. Any list we choose in the error post-model will have length less than two, and can then be used in the pre-model to reach the post-model. On the other hand, whichever list we choose in the success post-model will have length at least two, that is, its values will be of the form
v_{1}:v_{2}:\mathit{vs}
and it will have some addresses, and then we can choose a list with the same addresses and values
v_{2}:v_{1}:\mathit{vs}
in the pre-model and we will reach the post-model by executing the function.

Pointer-Reverse.

Let us now examine the list-pointer-reverse function,
\mathtt{LPRev}(\mathsf{x})
, which takes a list at address
\mathsf{x}
and reverses it by reversing the direction of the next-pointers, returning the head of the reversed list. Given (O2) and the fact that the algorithm manipulates pointers and returns an address, but the actual values in the list are not exposed, we will try to use the address-only
\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs})
predicate to specify this function as in the following OX triple, where
\mathit{xs}^{\dagger}
denotes the reverse of the mathematical list
\mathit{xs}
:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs})\end{array}\right\}}\ {~\mathtt{LPRev}(\mathsf{x})~}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{list}(\mathsf{ret},\mathit{xs}^{\dagger})\end{array}\right\}}

which would seem to be UX-valid given our OX experience and previous examples, but is not. In particular, it has no information about the logical variable
x
, which exists only in the pre-condition. This is not an issue in OX reasoning, but in UX reasoning it would mean that there exists a logical environment that interprets the post-condition but not the pre-condition, and such a specification, by the definition, could never be UX-valid.

To understand which specific information about
x
is required, we first add the general
x\in\mathsf{Val}
, making the post-condition
\mathsf{list}(\mathsf{ret},\mathit{xs}^{\dagger})\mathrel{\star}x\in\mathsf{Val}
, and then try to choose a post-model by picking values for
\mathsf{ret}
,
\mathit{xs}
, and
x
. Note that, given the definition of
\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs})
, we cannot just pick any non-correlated values for
\mathsf{ret}
and
\mathit{xs}
: in particular, either
\mathit{xs}
is an empty list and
\mathsf{ret}
is
\mathtt{null}
, or
\mathit{xs}
is non-empty and
\mathsf{ret}
is its last element. This observation, in fact, reveals the information needed about
x
: either
x
is
\mathtt{null}
and
\mathit{xs}
is empty, or
\mathit{xs}
is non-empty and
x
is its first element. We capture this information using the
\mathsf{listHead}(x,\mathit{xs})
predicate:

\mathsf{listHead}(x,\mathit{xs})\triangleq(\mathit{xs}=[~]\mathrel{\star}x=\mathtt{null})\lor(\exists\mathit{xs}^{\prime}\ldotp\mathit{xs}=x:\mathit{xs}^{\prime})

and arrive at the desired EX specification of the list-pointer-reverse algorithm:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs})\end{array})}\ {~\mathtt{LPRev}(\mathsf{x})~}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{list}(\mathsf{ret},\mathit{xs}^{\dagger})\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{listHead}(x,\mathit{xs})\end{array})}

Let us make sure that this specification is UX-valid. If we pick a post-model with
\mathit{xs}=[~]
, then
x=\mathsf{ret}=\mathtt{null}
and the pre-model with the same
x
and
\mathit{xs}
will work, as the list holds no values. For a post-model with non-empty
\mathit{xs}
,
x
must equal the head of
\mathit{xs}
,
\mathsf{ret}
must equal the tail of
\mathit{xs}
, and we also have to pick some arbitrary values
\mathit{vs}
, with
|\mathit{vs}|=|\mathit{xs}|
. Then, given the described behaviour of the algorithm, we know that this post-model is reachable from a pre-model which has the list at
x
with addresses
\mathit{xs}
and values
\mathit{vs}^{\dagger}
.

Free.

Next, we take a look at the
\mathsf{LFree}(\mathsf{x})
function, which frees a given list at address
\mathsf{x}
. Its OX specification is

\!\!\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(\mathsf{x})\end{array}\right\}}\ {\mathtt{LFree}(\mathsf{x})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{ret}=\mathtt{null}\end{array}\right\}}\end{array}
, but it does not transfer to UX contexts because no resource from the pre-condition can be forgotten in the post-condition as that would break the UX frame property
[
27
]
. Instead, we have to keep track of the addresses to be freed, which we can do using the
\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs})
predicate (or
\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs},\mathit{vs})
), and we also have to explicitly state in the post-condition that these addresses have been freed:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs})\end{array})}\ {~\mathtt{LFree}(\mathsf{x})~}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{freed}(\mathit{xs})\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{listHead}(x,\mathit{xs})\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=\mathtt{null}\end{array})}

using the
\mathsf{freed}(\mathit{xs})
predicate, which is defined as follows:

\mathsf{freed}(\mathit{xs})\triangleq(\mathit{xs}=[~])\lor(\exists x,\mathit{xs}^{\prime}\ldotp\mathit{xs}=x:\mathit{xs}^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}x\mapsto\varnothing,\varnothing\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{freed}(xs^{\prime}))

Client Programs and Specification Composition.

We discuss the usability of ESL specifications in general and abstraction in particular in the context of client programs that call multiple library functions. Consider the following (slightly contrived) client program, which takes a list and: pointer-reverses it if its length is between 5 and 10; frees it and then throws an error if its length is smaller than 5; and does not terminate otherwise:

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{LClient}(\mathsf{x})~\{\\
\quad\!\!\mathsf{l}\mathtt{\ :=\ }\mathtt{LLen(\mathsf{x})};\\
\quad\!\!\texttt{if}~(\mathsf{l}<5)\\
\quad\!\!\quad\!\!\{~\mathsf{r}\mathtt{\ :=\ }\mathtt{LFree(\mathsf{x})};~\mathtt{error}(\mathtt{``List~too~short!"})~\}~\mathtt{else}\\
\quad\!\!\quad\!\!\{~\mathtt{if}\ (\mathsf{l}>10)\ \{~\mathtt{while}\ ({\sf true})\ \{~\mathtt{skip}~\}~\}~\mathtt{else}~\{~\mathsf{r}\mathtt{\ :=\ }\mathtt{LPRev(\mathsf{l})}~\}~\};\\
\quad\!\!\mathtt{return}\ \mathsf{r}\\
\}\\
\end{array}

Our first goal is to understand which is the most abstract list predicate that could be used for reasoning about this client, since we want to minimise the amount of details we need to carry along in the proof, noting that the least abstract one,
\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs},\mathit{vs})
, will always work. Observe that, importantly, only specifications expressed at the same abstraction level are composable with each other, because they must be composed using equivalence. We explore this in more detail in the subsequent formal discussion (see, in particular, observation (O5)).

When it comes to
\mathtt{LClient}(\mathsf{x})
, for list-length, we need information about the list length, meaning that we can use either
\mathsf{list}(x,n)
,
\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs})
, or
\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{vs})
, but not
\mathsf{list}(x)
. For list-free, we must have information about the addresses, meaning that
\mathsf{list}(x,n)
and
\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{vs})
will not work, leaving us with
\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs})
, which is also usable for list-pointer-reverse. Therefore, we can write the specification of this client using the
\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs})
predicate, as follows:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs}))}\\
{\mathtt{LClient}(\mathsf{x})}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{ok}:5\mathbin{\leq}|\mathit{xs}|\mathbin{\leq}10\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(\mathsf{ret},\mathit{xs}^{\dagger})\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{listHead}(x,\mathit{xs}))}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:|\mathit{xs}|\mathbin{<}5\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{freed}(\mathit{xs})\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{listHead}(x,\mathit{xs})\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{err}=\mathtt{``List~too~short!"}\right)}\end{array}

In general, however, it is sufficient for a client to call one function that works with addresses and another that works with values for the only applicable predicate to be
\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs},\mathit{vs})
, which is still abstract in the sense that it allows for unbounded reasoning about lists, but does not hide any of its internal information. This leads us to the following observation:

(O3)
specifications that use predicates which hide data-structure information,

albeit provable, may have limited use in UX client reasoning.

As a final remark on abstraction, note that we have only considered predicates that expose the data-structure sub-parts (for lists, these sub-parts are values
\mathit{vs}
and addresses
\mathit{xs}
) either entirely or not at all. It would be also possible to expose
some
of this structure for some of the algorithms, but because of (O3), specifications using such abstractions are only composable with specifications exposing the same partial structure, and hence likely to be of limited use.

Non-termination.

We conclude our discussion on specifications with two remarks on EX reasoning about non-terminating behaviour. First, consider the non-terminating branch of the
LClient
function, which is triggered when
|\mathit{xs}|>10
. Observe that this branch is implicit in the client specification, in that it is subsumed by the success post-condition (since
\models P\lor(|\mathit{xs}|\mathbin{>}10\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{False})\Leftrightarrow P
). However, to demonstrate that it exists, we can constrain the pre-condition appropriately to prove the specification

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,\mathit{xs})\mathrel{\star}|\mathit{xs}|\mathbin{>}10\end{array})}\ {\mathtt{LClient}(\mathsf{x})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{False}\end{array})}
.
This implicit loss of non-terminating branches can be characterised informally as follows:

(O4)
if the post-conditions do not cover all paths allowed by the pre-condition,

then the “gap” is non-terminating.

In this case, the pre-condition implies
|\mathit{xs}|\in\mathbb{N}
and the post-conditions cover the cases where
|\mathit{xs}|\leq 10
, leaving the gap when
|\mathit{xs}|>10
, for which we provably have client non-termination.

Second, we observe that, in contrast to terminating behaviour, for non-terminating behaviour EX is as expressive as OX; that is, the EX triple
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array})}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{False}\end{array})}
is equivalent to the OX triple
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array}\right\}}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{False}\end{array}\right\}}
as the UX triple
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array}\right]}\ {\mathsf{C}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{False}\end{array}\right]}
is vacuously true. This is not to say that all non-terminating behaviour can be captured by ESL specifications. For example, as in OX, if the code branches on a value that does not come from the pre-condition, and if one of the resulting branches does not terminate, and if the code can also terminate successfully, then the non-terminating branch will be implicit in the pre-condition, but no gap in the sense of (O4) will be present. This is illustrated by the code and specification below, where the pre- and the post-condition are the same, but a non-terminating path still exists:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{x}=0\end{array}~)}~\mathsf{x}\mathtt{\ :=\ }\mathtt{nondet};~\mathtt{if}\ (\mathsf{x}>42)\ \{~\mathtt{while}\ ({\sf true})\ \{\mathtt{skip}\}~\}\ \mathtt{else}\ \{~\mathsf{x}\mathtt{\ :=\ }0~\}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{x}=0\end{array}~)}

5.3
More ESL Proofs: Iterative list-length

In §
2
, we have shown a proof sketch for a recursive implementation of the list-length algorithm, demonstrating how to handle the measure for recursive function calls; how the folding of predicates works in the presence of equivalence; and how to move between external and internal specifications. We highlight again the UX-specific issue that we raised and that is related to predicate folding, which can be formulated generally as follows:

(O5)
if the code accesses data-structure information that the used predicate hides, then that predicate might not be foldable in a UX-proof in all of the places in which

it would be foldable in the corresponding OX-proof.

Here, we show how to write ESL proofs for looping code, using as example an iterative implementation of the list-length algorithm. Proofs for the majority of the other algorithms mentioned in §
5.2
can be found in Appendix
F
; the rest are similar.

\small\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{~}l}\Gamma\vdash&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,n)\end{array}~)}\\
&\mathtt{LLen}(\mathsf{x})~\{\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,n)\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{r}=\mathtt{null}\end{array}~)}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\mathsf{r}\mathtt{\ :=\ }0\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,n)\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{r}=0\end{array}~)}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}{P_{0}}\end{array}~)}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\mathtt{while}\ (\mathsf{x}\neq\mathtt{null})\ \{\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}P_{i}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{x}\mathbin{\neq}\mathtt{null}\end{array}~)}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\exists j,v,x^{\prime}.~\mathsf{lseg}(x,\mathsf{x},i)\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{x}\mapsto v,x^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x^{\prime},j-1)\mathrel{\star}n=i+j\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{r}=i\end{array}~\right)}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\mathsf{x}\mathtt{\ :=\ }[\mathsf{x}+1];\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\exists j,v,x^{\prime}.~\mathsf{lseg}(x,x^{\prime},i)\mathrel{\star}x^{\prime}\mapsto v,\mathsf{x}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(\mathsf{x},j)\mathrel{\star}n=i+(j+1)\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{r}=i\end{array}~\right)}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\text{// equivalence: $\models\mathsf{lseg}(x,y,n+1)\Leftrightarrow\exists x^{\prime},v.~\mathsf{lseg}(x,x^{\prime},n)\mathrel{\star}x^{\prime}\mapsto v,y$]]}}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\exists j.~\mathsf{lseg}(x,\mathsf{x},i+1)\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(\mathsf{x},j)\mathrel{\star}n=(i+1)+j\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{r}=i\end{array}~\right)}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\mathsf{r}\mathtt{\ :=\ }\mathsf{r}+1\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}P_{i+1}\end{array}~)}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{x}=\mathtt{null}\mathrel{\star}\exists i.~P_{i}\end{array}~\right)}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\exists i,j.~\mathsf{lseg}(x,\mathsf{x},i)\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(\mathsf{x},j)\mathrel{\star}n=i+j\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{r}=i\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{x}=\mathtt{null}\end{array}~\right)}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{lseg}(x,\mathtt{null},n)\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{r}=n\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{x}=\mathtt{null}\end{array}~\right)}~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\text{//~equivalence: $\models\mathsf{list}(\mathtt{null},j)\Leftrightarrow j=0$}}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{list}(x,n)\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{r}=n\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{x}=\mathtt{null}\end{array}~\right)}~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\text{//~equivalence: $\models\mathsf{lseg}(x,\mathtt{null},n)\Leftrightarrow\mathsf{list}(x,n)$}}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!\mathtt{return}\ \mathsf{r}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{list}(x,n)\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{r}=n\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{x}=\mathtt{null}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=\mathsf{r}\end{array}~)}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\exists x_{q},r_{q}.~\mathsf{list}(x,n)\mathrel{\star}r_{q}=n\mathrel{\star}x_{q}=\mathtt{null}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=r_{q}\end{array}~)}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\!\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{list}(x,n)\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=n\end{array}~)}\\
&\}\\
&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(~\begin{array}[]{@{} l @{}}\mathsf{list}(x,n)\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=n\end{array}~)}\par\end{array}

Figure 4:
ESL proof sketch: iterative list-length.

Iterative list-length in ESL: Proof Sketch.

In Figure
4
, we give an iterative implementation of the list-length algorithm and show that it satisfies the same ESL specification as its recursive counterpart,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(x,n)\end{array})}\ {~\mathtt{LLen}(\mathsf{x})~}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(ok:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{list}(x,n)\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=n\end{array})}
. Since there is no recursion, we elide the (trivial) measure. To state the loop variant, we use the list-segment predicate, defined as follows:

\mathsf{lseg}(x,y,n)\triangleq(x=y\mathrel{\star}n=0)\lor(\exists v,x^{\prime}\ldotp x\mapsto v,x^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{lseg}(x^{\prime},y,n-1))

and to apply the
[while]
rule, we define:

P_{i}\triangleq\exists j.~\mathsf{lseg}(x,\mathsf{x},i)\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{list}(\mathsf{x},j)\mathrel{\star}n=i+j\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{r}=i

Note that we could have chosen to elide
i
from the body of
P_{i}
in this simple example, but since this is not necessarily possible or evident in general as well as for instructive purposes, we chose to keep it in the proof. Note how, on exiting the loop, the negation of the loop condition collapses the existentials
i
and
j
. This allows us to obtain the given internal post-condition, from which we then easily move to the desired external post-condition. For this proof, we also use three equivalence lemmas, which state that a non-empty list segment can be separated into its last element and the rest, that the length of an empty list equals zero, and that a null-terminated list-segment is a list.

5.4
Beyond List Examples: Binary Search Trees

While list algorithms illustrate many aspects of exact reasoning, it is also important to understand how ESL specification and verification works with other data structures. For this reason, we discuss two algorithms operating over binary search trees (BSTs) that are intended to represent sets of natural numbers. We use two abstractions for BSTs, one in which only their values are considered as a mathematical set:

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{~}l}\mathsf{BST}(x,K)\triangleq&(x=\mathtt{null}\mathrel{\star}K=\emptyset)\lor(\exists k,l,r,K_{l},K_{r}\ldotp x\mapsto k,l,r\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{BST}(r,K_{r})\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{BST}(l,K_{l})\mathrel{\star}\\
&\qquad K=K_{l}\uplus\{k\}\uplus K_{r}\mathrel{\star}K_{l}<k\mathrel{\star}k<K_{r})\end{array}

and another that fully exposes the BST structure:

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{~}l}\mathsf{BST}(x,\tau)\triangleq&(x=\mathtt{null}\mathrel{\star}\tau=\tau_{\emptyset})\lor(\exists k,l,r,\tau_{l},\tau_{r}\ldotp E\mapsto k,l,r\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{BST}(r,\tau_{r})\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{BST}(l,\tau_{l})\mathrel{\star}\\
&\qquad\tau=((x,k),\tau_{l},\tau_{r})\mathrel{\star}\tau_{l}<k\mathrel{\star}k<\tau_{r})\end{array}

where
\tau
is a mathematical tree, that is, an algebraic data type with two constructors representing, respectively, an empty tree and a root node with two child trees:
\tau\in\mathsf{Tree}\triangleq\tau_{\emptyset}~|~((x,n),\tau_{l},\tau_{r})
,
where the notation
(x,n)
represents a BST node with address
x
and value
n
. Note the overloaded
<
notation, where one of the operands can be a set or a tree, which carry the intuitive meaning.

BST algorithms.
We first consider the BST-find-minimum algorithm,
\mathtt{BSTFindMin}(\mathsf{x})
, which takes a tree with root at
\mathsf{x}
, does not modify it, and returns its minimum element or throws an empty-tree error. Since that algorithm operates only on the values in the tree, we are able to state its ESL specification using the
\mathsf{BST}(x,K)
predicate as follows:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{BST}(x,K))}~{\mathtt{BSTFindMin}(\mathsf{x})}\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{ok}{:}~x\neq\mathtt{null}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{BST}(x,K)\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=\min(K))}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{err}{:}~x=\mathtt{null}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{BST}(x,K)\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{err}=\mathtt{``Empty~tree!"})}\end{array}\end{array}

We have also considered the BST-insert algorithm,
\mathtt{BSTInsert}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{v})
, which takes a tree with root at
\mathsf{x}
and inserts a new node with value
\mathsf{v}
into it as a leaf if
\mathsf{v}
is not already in the tree, or leaves the tree unmodified if it is. As this algorithm interacts both with values and addresses in the tree, the appropriate abstraction for it is
\mathsf{BST}(x,\tau)
, and its ESL specification is:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathsf{x}=x\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{v}=v\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{BST}(x,\tau))}\\
{\mathtt{BSTInsert}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{v})}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\exists x^{\prime}.~\mathsf{BST}(\mathsf{ret},\mathsf{BSTInsert}(\tau,(x^{\prime},v)))\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{BSTRoot}(x,\tau))}\end{array}

where
\mathsf{BSTInsert}(\tau,\nu)
is the mathematical algorithm that inserts the node
\nu
into the tree
\tau
:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\qquad}l}\mathsf{BSTInsert}(\tau_{\emptyset},(x^{\prime},v))\triangleq&\mathsf{BSTInsert}(((x,k),\tau_{l},\tau_{r}),(x^{\prime},v))\triangleq\\
\qquad((x^{\prime},v),\tau_{\emptyset},\tau_{\emptyset})&\qquad\mathsf{if}\ v<k\ \mathsf{then}~((x,k),\mathsf{BSTInsert}(\tau_{l},(x^{\prime},v)),\tau_{r})\\
&\qquad\mathsf{else\ if}\ k<v\ \mathsf{then}~((x,k),\tau_{l},\mathsf{BSTInsert}(\tau_{r},(x^{\prime},v)))\\
&\qquad\mathsf{else}((x,k),\tau_{l},\tau_{r})\end{array}

and the predicate
\mathsf{BSTRoot}(x,\tau)
is defined analogously to
\mathsf{listHead}(x,\mathit{xs})
:

\mathsf{BSTRoot}(x,\tau)\triangleq(\tau=\tau_{\emptyset}\mathrel{\star}x=\mathtt{null})\lor(\exists k,\tau_{l},\tau_{r}\ldotp\tau=((x,k),\tau_{l},\tau_{r}))

This example shows how in EX verification, just as in OX verification, we end up relating an imperative heap-manipulating algorithm to its mathematical/functional counterpart (cf. Appel
[
3
]
for a recent reiteration of this idea). The additional work required is that EX mathematical models must be more detailed: we are, yet again, not allowed to lose information. In particular, in OX verification we could relate
\mathtt{BSTInsert}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{v})
to mathematical sets, but in EX verification we must relate our imperative implementation to tree models, including both values and pointers. Moreover, our mathematical model of the algorithm,
\mathsf{BSTInsert}(\tau,(x^{\prime},v))
, must insert elements in the same way as the imperative implementation, that is, in this case at the leaves of the tree. The proofs for both algorithms are given in Appendix
F
.

6
Related Work

In the previous sections, we have placed ESL in the context of related work on OX and UX logics and associated tools. Here, we discuss formalisms capable of reasoning both about program correctness and program incorrectness, as well as existing approaches to the use of function specifications (summaries) and abstraction in symbolic execution.

Program Logics for Both Correctness and Incorrectness.

Developed in parallel but independently of ESL, Outcome Logic (OL)
[
32
]
, much like ESL, brings together reasoning about correctness and incorrectness into one logic. Both OL and ESL rely on the traditional meaning of correctness, but OL introduces a new approach to incorrectness, based on reachability of sets of states. It has not yet been shown that this approach has the same bug-finding potential as that of ISL: in particular, bi-abduction has not yet been demonstrated to be compatible with OL. In addition, the OL work, in contrast to ESL, does not discuss function compositionality or the interaction between abstraction, reachability, and incorrectness.

LCL
A

[
4
,
5
]
is a non-function-compositional, first-order logic that combines UX and OX reasoning using abstract interpretation. It is parametric on an abstract domain
A
, and proves UX triples of the form
\vdash_{A}[P]~\mathsf{C}~[Q]
where, under certain conditions, the triple also guarantees verification. These conditions, however, normally mean that only a limited number of pre-conditions can be handled. The conditions also have to be checked per-command and if they fail to hold (due to, e.g., issues with Boolean guards, which are known to be a major source of incompleteness), then the abstract domain has to be incrementally adjusted; the complexity of this adjustment and the expressivity of the resulting formalism is unclear.

Compositional Symbolic Execution.

There exists a substantial body of work on symbolic execution with function summaries (e.g.
[
15
,
1
,
16
,
22
,
17
,
31
]
), which is primarily based on first-order logic.
We highlight the work of Godefroid et al., which initially used exact summaries of bounded program behaviour to drive the compositional dynamic test generation of SMART
[
15
]
, and later distinguished between may (OX) and must (UX) summaries, leveraging the interaction between them to design the SMASH tool for compositional property checking and test generation
[
16
]
. SMASH, however, is limited in its ability to reason about heap-manipulating programs because, for example, it lacks support for pointer arithmetic. Nevertheless, it shows that interactions between OX and UX summaries can be exploited for automation, which is an important consideration for any automation of ESL. For example, SMASH is able to use not-may summaries (which amount to non-reachability) when constructing must-summaries (which amount to reachability), using the former to restrict the latter.
When it comes to abstraction, for example, Anand et al.
[
2
]
implement linked-list and array abstractions for true bug-finding in non-compositional symbolic execution, in the context of the Java PathFinder, and use it to find bugs in list and array partitioning algorithms. True bug-finding is maintained by checking for state subsumption, which requires code modification rather than annotation and a record of all previously visited states.

7
Conclusions

We have introduced ESL, a program logic for exact reasoning about
heap-manipulating programs. ESL specifications provide a sweet spot
between verification and true bug-finding: as SL specifications, they capture all terminating behaviour, and, as
ISL specifications, they describe only results and errors that are
reachable. ESL specifications are therefore compatible with
tools that target OX
verification, such as VeriFast
[
19
]
and
Iris
[
20
]
,
tools that target UX true
bug-finding, such as Pulse
[
25
,
27
]
, and tools capable of
targeting both, such as Gillian
[
10
,
23
]
.
ESL supports reasoning about
mutually recursive functions and comes with a soundness result that
immediately transfers to SL and ISL, thus demonstrating, for the
first time, scalable functional compositionality for UX logics.

We have verified exact specifications for a number of illustrative examples, showing that ESL can reason about data-structure libraries, language errors, mutual recursion, and non-termination. In doing so, we emphasise the distinction between the often-conflated concepts of abstraction and over-approximation. We have demonstrated that abstract predicates can be soundly used in EX and UX reasoning, albeit not as freely as in OX reasoning.

We believe that ESL reasoning, in its intended context of
semi-automatic verification of functional correctness properties,
is useful for the verification of self-contained, critical code that underpins a larger
codebase.
To demonstrate this, we will in the future incorporate UX
and EX verification inside
Gillian
[
10
,
23
]
,
which already has support for function compositionality and
semi-automatic predicate management as part of its OX
verification.

Appendix A
Complete Operational Semantics

s_{\mathit{err}}\triangleq s[\mathsf{err}\rightarrow v_{\mathit{err}}]

Appendix B
Exact Separation Logic

The complete rules of ESL are as follows (with
Q_{\mathit{err}}^{*}\triangleq\mathsf{pre}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{err}=\mathit{E}_{\mathit{err}}
):

\frac{}{\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\sf emp}\end{array})}\ {\texttt{skip}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\sf emp}\end{array})}}\hskip Q_{\mathit{err}}^{\prime}[\vec{z}/\vec{y}]\end{array}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathit{ok}:Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathit{err}:Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array})}\in\mathcal{A}\mkern-1.0mu\mathit{dm}(T)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{\mbox{adm-alpha-equiv}}

These admissibility rules are validity-preserving and they lift the logic rules
frame
,
exists
,
equiv
and
disj
to the level of function specifications, as well as allow for the renaming of logical variables. These are commonly implicitly used in OX logics.

The
env-extend
rule in the figure above differs in several ways from the rule given in the main body (§
4.3
):

\small\frac{{\begin{array}[]{l}\vdash(\gamma,\Gamma)\hskip 18.49988ptf\not\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}(\gamma)\hskip 18.49988pt\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma[f\mapsto(\vec{\mathsf{x}},\mathsf{C},\mathsf{E})]\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{array}[]{@{}ll}t\triangleq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array})}&t_{\infty}\triangleq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P_{\infty}\end{array})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{False}\end{array})}\hskip 9.24994ptt(\alpha)\triangleq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\mathrel{\star}\alpha=E_{\mu}\end{array})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\mathrel{\star}\alpha=E_{\mu}\end{array})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\mathrel{\star}\alpha=E_{\mu}\end{array})}&t_{\infty}(\alpha)\triangleq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P_{\infty}\mathrel{\star}\alpha=E_{\mu}\end{array})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{False}\end{array})}\end{array}\hskip 9.24994pt~\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma(\alpha)=\Gamma[f\mapsto\{t(\beta)\mid{\beta<\alpha}\}\mathbin{\cup}\{t_{\infty}\mid\beta\leq\alpha\}]\hskip 9.24994pt\forall\alpha\ldotp\exists t^{\prime}\in\mathit{Int}_{\gamma^{\prime},f}\big(t(\alpha)\big).~\Gamma(\alpha)\vdash\mathsf{C}:t^{\prime}\hskip 18.49988pt{\forall\alpha\ldotp\exists t^{\prime}\in\mathit{Int}_{\gamma^{\prime},f}(t_{\infty}(\alpha)).~\Gamma(\alpha)\vdash\mathsf{C}:t^{\prime}}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma^{\prime}:=\Gamma[f\mapsto\{t,t_{\infty}\}]\end{array}}}{\vdash(\gamma^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime})}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{\mbox{env-extend}}

The more complex rule allows us to reason about a broader set of functions, and we chose to present a simpler version in the body of the paper for didactical reasons.
The proof of soundness conducted in Appendix
E
proves soundness of the more complex rule,
which has the following strengths over the simplified version:

•

Instead of adding only one function
f
, we add the finite set
\{f_{i}~|~i=1,..,n\}
, allowing us to reason about
mutally recursive
functions.

•

We extend the measure beyond natural numbers to computable ordinals,
\mathcal{O}\triangleq\omega_{1}^{\text{CK}}
, allowing us to reason about a broader set of functions, such as those with non-deterministic nested recursion.

•

We use the more general notation
P(\alpha)
instead of
P\mathrel{\star}\alpha=\mathit{E}_{\mu}
, and similarly for post-conditions. For this reason, we require an explicit transition from
\alpha
being a meta-variable to it being a logical variable, as in the while rule. It can then be removed from the specification using admissible equivalence.

Appendix C
Soundness: ESL

In order to prove the soundness result of theorem
17
, we require three auxiliary lemmas regarding environment validity. Their proofs are straightforward and will therefore be omitted. For legacy reasons, this appendix and the following appendices may use an alternative notation for satisfiability:
\theta,s,h\models P
instead of
\theta,(s,h)\models P
, where
\theta
(referred to as the
substitution
) is of type:
\mathsf{LVar}\rightharpoonup_{\mathtt{fin}}\mathsf{Val}
.

Lemma 19

(Auxiliary properties)
.

The following properties hold:

1.

\forall\theta,s,s^{\prime},h,P\ldotp\theta,s,h\models P\wedge s|_{\mathtt{pv}(P)}=s^{\prime}|_{\mathtt{pv}(P)}\Rightarrow\theta,s^{\prime},h\models P

2.

\forall\theta,s,s^{\prime},h,h^{\prime},o,{C},\gamma\ldotp(s,h),{C}\Downarrow_{\gamma}o:(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})\implies\forall x\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}(s)\setminus\mathtt{mod}(C)\ldotp s(x)=s^{\prime}(x)

3.

\forall\mathsf{E},\theta,s,s^{\prime},\mathsf{y}\ldotp s|_{\mathtt{pv}(\mathsf{E})\backslash\{\mathsf{y}\}}=s^{\prime}|_{\mathtt{pv}(\mathsf{E})\backslash\{\mathsf{y}\}}\Rightarrow\llbracket{\mathsf{E}[s^{\prime}(\mathsf{y})/\mathsf{y}]}\rrbracket_{\theta,s}=\llbracket{\mathsf{E}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s^{\prime}}

We also require a lemma that states that environment validity is closed under admissibility:

Lemma 20

(Admissibility and Environment Validity)
.

If
\models(\gamma,\Gamma)
, then
\models(\gamma,\mathcal{A}\mkern-1.0mu\mathit{dm}(\Gamma))
, where
\mathcal{A}\mkern-1.0mu\mathit{dm}(\Gamma)=\{f\mapsto\mathcal{A}\mkern-1.0mu\mathit{dm}(\Gamma(f))\mid f\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}(\Gamma)\}
.

Proof.

Assume that
\models(\gamma,\Gamma)
. It is sufficient to prove the claim for an isolated
f\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}(\Gamma)
, by induction on the formation of
\mathcal{A}\mkern-1.0mu\mathit{dm}(\Gamma(f))
. The proof of the four non-trivial cases is analogous to the proof of the respective structural rules in the proof of Theorem
17
, given below.
∎

Proof of Theorem
17
.

By induction on the derivation
\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\end{array}\right)}\ {C}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}\right)}
. We prove a representative selection of rules; the proofs for the remaining ones are analogous.

Function Call.
We first prove the successfully terminating case of under-approximation. Our hypotheses are:

(H1)

\models(\gamma,\Gamma)
,

(H2)

\theta,s^{\prime},h^{\prime}\models\mathit{E}_{y}\mathbin{\in}\mathsf{Val}\mathrel{\star}\vec{\mathsf{E}}[\mathit{E}_{y}/\mathsf{y}]=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}Q_{\mathit{ok}}[\mathsf{y}/\mathtt{ret}]
,

(H3)

{h^{\prime}\mathbin{\sharp}h_{f}}
,

(H5)

\mathsf{y}\not\in\mathtt{fv}(\mathit{E}_{y})
,

(H6)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}\right)}\in\mathcal{A}\mkern-1.0mu\mathit{dm}(\Gamma(f))

Our goal is to show that:

\exists s,h\ldotp\theta,s,h\models\mathsf{y}=\mathit{E}_{y}\mathrel{\star}\vec{\mathsf{E}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P\wedge(s,h\uplus h_{f}),\mathsf{y}\mathtt{\ :=\ }f(\vec{\mathsf{x}})\Downarrow_{\gamma}ok:(s^{\prime},h^{\prime}\uplus h_{f})

•

From (H1) and Lemma
20
, we obtain
(H1a)

\models(\gamma,\mathcal{A}\mkern-1.0mu\mathit{dm}(\Gamma))

•

From (H6) and (H1a), we obtain
C
and
\mathsf{E}
, such that
(H7)

f(\vec{\mathsf{x}})\{C;\mathtt{return}\ \mathsf{E}\}\in\gamma
.

•

From (H2), we obtain that
(H2a)

\theta,s^{\prime}\models\vec{\mathsf{E}}[\mathit{E}_{y}/\mathsf{y}]=\vec{x}
,
and
(H2b)

\theta,s^{\prime},h^{\prime}\models Q_{\mathit{ok}}[\mathsf{y}/\mathsf{ret}]
.

•

From (H1a), (H6) and (H7), we obtain that there exists a specification

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P\mathrel{\star}\vec{\mathsf{z}}=\mathtt{null}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}^{\prime}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}^{\prime}\end{array}\right)}\in\mathit{Int}_{\gamma,f}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}\right)})

such that
(H8)

\gamma\models{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P\mathrel{\star}\vec{\mathsf{z}}=\mathtt{null}\end{array}\right)}\ {C}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}^{\prime}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}^{\prime}\end{array}\right)}
, where, from the definition of the internalisation function, we know that
(H9a)

Q_{\mathit{ok}}\Leftrightarrow\exists\vec{p}\ldotp Q_{\mathit{ok}}^{\prime}[\vec{p}/\vec{\mathsf{p}}]\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=\mathsf{E}[\vec{p}/\vec{\mathsf{p}}]
, where
\vec{\mathsf{z}}=\mathtt{pv}(C)\backslash\{\vec{\mathsf{x}}\}
and
\vec{\mathsf{p}}=\{\vec{\mathsf{x}}\}\uplus\{\vec{\mathsf{z}}\}=\mathtt{pv}(C)
.

•

Given (H2b) and (H9a), we derive the following:

\begin{array}[]{l r}\theta,s^{\prime},h^{\prime}\models(\exists\vec{p}\ldotp Q_{\mathit{ok}}^{\prime}[\vec{p}/\vec{\mathsf{p}}]\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{ret}=\mathsf{E}[\vec{p}/\vec{\mathsf{p}}])[\mathsf{y}/\mathsf{ret}]&\\
\Rightarrow\theta,s^{\prime},h^{\prime}\models\exists\vec{p}\ldotp Q_{\mathit{ok}}^{\prime}[\vec{p}/\vec{\mathsf{p}}]\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{y}=\mathsf{E}[\vec{p}/\vec{\mathsf{p}}]&\\
\end{array}

from which we obtain that there exist values
\vec{w}
, such that:

\begin{array}[]{l r}\Rightarrow\theta[\vec{p}\rightarrow\vec{w}],s^{\prime},h^{\prime}\models Q_{\mathit{ok}}^{\prime}[\vec{p}/\vec{\mathsf{p}}]\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{y}=\mathsf{E}[\vec{p}/\vec{\mathsf{p}}]&\\
\Rightarrow\theta[\vec{p}\rightarrow\vec{w}],s^{\prime},h^{\prime}\models Q_{\mathit{ok}}^{\prime}[\vec{w}/\vec{\mathsf{p}}]\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{y}=\mathsf{E}[\vec{w}/\vec{\mathsf{p}}]&\\
\Rightarrow\theta[\vec{p}\rightarrow\vec{w}],s^{\prime}[\vec{\mathsf{p}}\rightarrow\vec{w}],h^{\prime}\models Q_{\mathit{ok}}^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{y}=\mathsf{E}&\\
\Rightarrow\theta,s^{\prime}[\vec{\mathsf{p}}\rightarrow\vec{w}],h^{\prime}\models Q_{\mathit{ok}}^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{y}=\mathsf{E}&\textbf{(H10a)}\\
\Rightarrow\theta,s^{\prime}[\vec{\mathsf{p}}\rightarrow\vec{w}],h^{\prime}\models Q_{\mathit{ok}}^{\prime}&\textbf{(H10b)}\\
\end{array}

•

Instantiating (H8) with (H10b) and (H3), we obtain that there exist
\tilde{s}
and
h
, such that

(H11)

\theta,\tilde{s},h\models\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P\mathrel{\star}\vec{\mathsf{z}}=\mathtt{null}
and

(H12)

(\tilde{s},h\uplus h_{f}),C\Downarrow_{\gamma}ok:(s^{\prime}[\vec{\mathsf{p}}\rightarrow\vec{w}],h^{\prime}\uplus h_{f})
.

•

Let
\vec{v}=\theta(\vec{x})
. Then, since
\mathtt{pv}(P)=\emptyset
and given Lemma
19
(
1
), taking
s^{\prime\prime}:=\emptyset[\vec{\mathsf{x}}\rightarrow\vec{v}][\vec{\mathsf{z}}\rightarrow\mathtt{null}]
, we obtain that
(H13)

\theta,s^{\prime\prime},h\models\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P\mathrel{\star}\vec{\mathsf{z}}=\mathtt{null}
and also that
(H14)

(s^{\prime\prime},h\uplus h_{f}),C\Downarrow_{\gamma}ok:(s^{\prime}[\vec{\mathsf{p}}\rightarrow\vec{w}],h^{\prime}\uplus h_{f})
.

•

Let
v^{\prime}=\llbracket{\mathsf{E}}\rrbracket_{s^{\prime}[\vec{\mathsf{p}}\rightarrow\vec{w}]}=\llbracket{\mathsf{E}[\vec{w}/\vec{\mathsf{p}}]}\rrbracket_{\theta,s^{\prime}}
,
v_{y}=\llbracket{\mathit{E}_{y}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s^{\prime}}
and
(H15)

s=s^{\prime}[\mathsf{y}\rightarrow v_{y}]
. Therefore, we also have that
(H16)

s^{\prime}=s[\mathsf{y}\rightarrow v^{\prime}]
.

•

We now need to prove that
(s,h\uplus h_{f}),\mathsf{y}\mathtt{\ :=\ }f(\vec{\mathsf{E}})\Downarrow_{\gamma}ok:(s[\mathsf{y}\rightarrow v^{\prime}],h^{\prime}\uplus h_{f})
. For this, we already have:

f(\vec{\mathsf{x}})\,\{\,{C};\mathtt{return}\ \mathsf{E}\,\}\in\gamma
,
\mathtt{pv}({C})\setminus\{\vec{\mathsf{x}}\}=\{\vec{\mathsf{z}}\}
,
s^{\prime\prime}=\emptyset[\vec{\mathsf{x}}\rightarrow\vec{v}][\vec{\mathsf{z}}\rightarrow\mathtt{null}]
,
(s^{\prime\prime},h\uplus h_{f}),{C}\Downarrow_{\gamma}({s^{\prime}},{h^{\prime}\uplus h^{\prime}_{f}})
and
\llbracket{\mathsf{E}}\rrbracket_{s^{\prime}[\vec{\mathsf{p}}\rightarrow\vec{w}]}=v^{\prime}
,
and we still need
\llbracket{\vec{{E}}}\rrbracket_{s}=\vec{v}
.
Rewriting (H2a) given (H16), we get
\theta,s[\mathsf{y}\rightarrow v^{\prime}]\models\vec{\mathsf{E}}[\mathit{E}_{y}/\mathsf{y}]=\vec{x}
, that is,
(H17)

\llbracket{\vec{\mathsf{E}}[\mathit{E}_{y}/\mathsf{y}]}\rrbracket_{\theta,s[\mathsf{y}\rightarrow v^{\prime}]}=~\vec{v}
.
From (H17), the definition of
s
, and Lemma
19
(
3
), we then obtain that
\llbracket{\vec{{E}}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s}=\vec{v}
, and from there, as
\vec{\mathsf{E}}
are program expressions, we obtain the desired
\llbracket{\vec{{E}}}\rrbracket_{s}=\vec{v}
.

•

Finally, we need to prove that
\theta,s,h\models\mathsf{y}=\mathit{E}_{y}\mathrel{\star}\vec{\mathsf{E}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P
. For the first “starjunct”, we need to show
\llbracket{\mathsf{y}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s}=\llbracket{\mathit{E}_{y}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s}
:

\llbracket{\mathsf{y}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s}=s(\mathsf{y})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(H15)}}}{{=}}(s^{\prime}[\mathsf{y}\rightarrow v_{y}])(\mathsf{y})=v_{y}=\llbracket{\mathit{E}_{y}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(H16)}}}{{=}}\llbracket{\mathit{E}_{y}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s[\mathsf{y}\rightarrow v^{\prime}]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(H5)}}}{{=}}\llbracket{\mathit{E}_{y}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s}

For the second starjunct we do:

\llbracket{\vec{x}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s}=\theta(\vec{x})=\llbracket{\vec{x}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(H2a)}}}{{=}}\llbracket{\vec{\mathsf{E}}[\mathit{E}_{y}/\mathsf{y}]}\rrbracket_{\theta,s^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(H15)}}}{{\ref{lem:auxprop}.\ref{LemSubst}}}{=}\llbracket{\vec{\mathsf{E}}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s}

The third starjunct follows from (H13), given that
P
has no logical variables by construction.

Next, we prove the erroneously terminating case of under-approximation. Our hypotheses are:

(H1)

\models(\gamma,\Gamma)
,

(H2)

\theta,s^{\prime},h^{\prime}\models\mathsf{y}=\mathit{E}_{y}\mathrel{\star}\vec{\mathsf{E}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}Q_{\mathit{err}}
,

(H3)

{h^{\prime}\mathbin{\sharp}h_{f}}
,

(H4)

\mathsf{y}\not\in\mathtt{fv}(\mathit{E}_{y})
,

(H5)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}\right)}\in\mathcal{A}\mkern-1.0mu\mathit{dm}(\Gamma(f))
.

Our goal is to show that:

\exists s,h\ldotp\theta,s,h\models\mathsf{y}=\mathit{E}_{y}\mathrel{\star}\vec{\mathsf{E}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P\wedge(s,h\uplus h_{f}),\mathsf{y}\mathtt{\ :=\ }f(\vec{\mathsf{x}})\Downarrow_{\gamma}err:(s^{\prime},h^{\prime}\uplus h_{f})

•

From (H1) and Lemma
20
, we obtain
(H1a)

\models(\gamma,\mathcal{A}\mkern-1.0mu\mathit{dm}(\Gamma))

•

From (H5) and (H1a), we obtain
C
and
\mathsf{E}
, such that
(H7)

f(\vec{\mathsf{x}})\{C;\mathtt{return}\ \mathsf{E}\}\in\gamma
.

•

From (H2), we obtain that
(H2a)

\theta,s^{\prime}\models\vec{\mathsf{E}}=\vec{x}
,
(H2b)

\theta,s^{\prime},h^{\prime}\models Q_{\mathit{err}}
and
(H2c)

\theta,s^{\prime}\models\mathsf{y}=\mathit{E}_{y}
.

•

From (H1a), (H5) and (H7), we obtain that there exists a specification

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P\mathrel{\star}\vec{\mathsf{z}}=\mathtt{null}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}^{\prime}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}^{\prime}\end{array}\right)}\in\mathit{Int}_{\gamma,f}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}\right)})

such that
(H8)

\gamma\models{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P\mathrel{\star}\vec{\mathsf{z}}=\mathtt{null}\end{array}\right)}\ {C}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}^{\prime}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}^{\prime}\end{array}\right)}
, where, from the definition of the internalisation function, we know that
(H9a)

Q_{\mathit{err}}\Leftrightarrow\exists\vec{p}\ldotp Q_{\mathit{err}}^{\prime}[\vec{p}/\vec{\mathsf{p}}]
with
\vec{\mathsf{z}}=\mathtt{pv}(C)\backslash\{\vec{\mathsf{x}}\}
and
\vec{\mathsf{p}}=\{\vec{\mathsf{x}}\}\uplus\{\vec{\mathsf{z}}\}
.

•

Given (H2b) and (H9a), analogous to the previous case we obtain:

\theta,s^{\prime},h^{\prime}\models(\exists\vec{p}\ldotp Q_{\mathit{err}}^{\prime}[\vec{p}/\vec{\mathsf{p}}])\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\exists\vec{w}\ldotp\theta,s_{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime},h^{\prime}\models Q_{\mathit{err}}^{\prime}\quad\textbf{(H10)}

where
s_{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}=s^{\prime}[\vec{\mathsf{p}}\mapsto\vec{w}]

•

Instantiating (H8) with (H1), (H10), and (H3), we obtain that there exist
\tilde{s}
and
h
, such that

(H11)

\theta,\tilde{s},h\models\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P\mathrel{\star}\vec{\mathsf{z}}=\mathtt{null}
and

(H12)

(\tilde{s},h\uplus h_{f}),C\Downarrow_{\gamma}err:(s_{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime},h^{\prime}\uplus h_{f})
.

•

Let
\vec{v}=\theta(\vec{x})
. Then, since
\mathtt{pv}(P)=\emptyset
and given Lemma
19
(
1
), taking
s^{\prime\prime}:=\emptyset[\vec{\mathsf{x}}\rightarrow\vec{v}][\vec{\mathsf{z}}\rightarrow\mathtt{null}]
, we obtain that
(H13)

\theta,s^{\prime\prime},h\models\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P\mathrel{\star}\vec{\mathsf{z}}=\mathtt{null}
and also that
(H14)

(s^{\prime\prime},h\uplus h_{f}),C\Downarrow_{\gamma}err:(s_{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime},h^{\prime}\uplus h_{f})
.

•

Let

v_{err}=\llbracket{\mathsf{err}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s^{\prime}}
and
(H15)

s=s^{\prime}\backslash\mathsf{err}
. Therefore, we also have that
(H16)

s^{\prime}=s[\mathsf{err}\rightarrow v_{err}]
.

•

We now need to prove that
(s,h\uplus h_{f}),\mathsf{y}\mathtt{\ :=\ }f(\vec{\mathsf{E}})\Downarrow_{\gamma}err:(s[\mathsf{err}\rightarrow v_{err}],h^{\prime}\uplus h_{f})
. For this, we already have:

f(\vec{\mathsf{x}})\,\{\,{C};\mathtt{return}\ \mathsf{E}\,\}\in\gamma
,
\mathtt{pv}({C})\setminus\{\vec{\mathsf{x}}\}=\{\vec{\mathsf{z}}\}
,
s^{\prime\prime}=\emptyset[\vec{\mathsf{x}}\rightarrow\vec{v}][\vec{\mathsf{z}}\rightarrow\mathtt{null}]
and
(s^{\prime\prime},h\uplus h_{f}),{C}\Downarrow_{\gamma}err:({s^{\prime}},{h^{\prime}\uplus h^{\prime}_{f}})
,
and we still need
\llbracket{\vec{{E}}}\rrbracket_{s}=\vec{v}
.
Rewriting (H2a) given (H16), we get
\theta,s^{\prime}\models\vec{\mathsf{E}}=\vec{x}
, that is,
(H17)

\llbracket{\vec{\mathsf{E}}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s^{\prime}}=~\vec{v}
.
From (H16) and (H17), we then obtain that
\llbracket{\vec{{E}}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s}=\vec{v}
, which yields
\llbracket{\vec{{E}}}\rrbracket_{s}=\vec{v}
since
\mathsf{E}
is a program expression.

•

Finally, we need to prove that
\theta,s,h\models\mathsf{y}=\mathit{E}_{y}\mathrel{\star}\vec{\mathsf{E}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P
.
The first starjunct is proven as follows:

\llbracket{\mathsf{y}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s}=s(\mathsf{y})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(H16)}}}{{=}}s^{\prime}(\mathsf{y})=\llbracket{\mathit{E}_{y}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(H16)}}}{{=}}\llbracket{\mathit{E}_{y}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s}

For the second starjunct we do:

\llbracket{\vec{x}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s}=\theta(\vec{x})=\llbracket{\vec{x}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(H2a)}}}{{=}}\llbracket{\vec{\mathsf{E}}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(H15)}}}{{=}}\llbracket{\vec{\mathsf{E}}}\rrbracket_{\theta,s}

The third starjunct follows from (H13), given that
P
has no logical variables by construction.

We move on to proving the successfully terminating over-approximation soundness. Our hypotheses are:

(H1)

\models(\gamma,\Gamma)

(H2)

\theta,s,h\models\mathsf{y}=\mathit{E}_{y}\mathrel{\star}\vec{\mathsf{E}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P

(H3)

h~\sharp~h_{f}

(H4)

\mathsf{y}\not\in\mathtt{fv}(\mathit{E}_{y})

(H5)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{ok}}\end{array}\right)}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q_{\mathit{err}}\end{array}\right)}\in\mathcal{A}\mkern-1.0mu\mathit{dm}(\Gamma(f))
.

Our goal is to show that:

\begin{array}[]{l}\forall s^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}\ldotp(s,h\uplus h_{f}),\mathsf{y}\mathtt{\ :=\ }f(\vec{\mathsf{x}})\Downarrow_{\gamma}ok:(s^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime})\\
\quad\quad\Rightarrow(o\neq\mathit{miss}\land\exists h^{\prime}\ldotp h^{\prime\prime}=h^{\prime}\uplus h_{f}\wedge\theta,s^{\prime},h^{\prime}\models\vec{\mathsf{E}}[\mathit{E}_{y}/\mathsf{y}]=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}Q_{\mathit{ok}}[\mathsf{y}/\mathsf{ret}])\end{array}

•

From (H1) and Lemma
20
, we obtain
(H1a)

\models(\gamma,\mathcal{A}\mkern-1.0mu\mathit{dm}(\Gamma))

•

From (H1a) and (H5), we obtain
C
and
\mathsf{E}
such that
(H6)

f(\vec{\mathsf{x}})\{C,\mathtt{return}\ \mathsf{E}\}\in\gamma
.

•

We define
\vec{v}:=\theta(\vec{x})
and obtain from (H2) that
(H2a)

\theta,s\models\vec{\mathsf{E}}=\vec{x}
and
\theta,s,h\models P
, and hence
(H2b)

\theta,s[\vec{\mathsf{x}}\rightarrow\vec{v}],h\models\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P
.

•

Since
\mathtt{pv}(P)=\emptyset
we obtain from (H2b) with Lemma
1
that
\theta,\emptyset[\vec{\mathsf{x}}\rightarrow\vec{v}],h\models\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P
and hence
(H7)

\theta,\emptyset[\vec{\mathsf{x}}\rightarrow\vec{v}][\vec{\mathsf{z}}\rightarrow\mathtt{null}],h\models\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P\mathrel{\star}\vec{\mathsf{z}}=\mathtt{null}
.

•
